Good-for-Game QPTL: An Alternating Hodges Semantics
Dylan Bellier, Massimo Benerecetti, Dario Della Monica, Fabio Mogavero

Abstract
An extension of Q PTL is considered where functional dependencies among the quantified variables can be restricted in such a way that their current values are independent of the future values of the other variables. This restriction is tightly connected to the notion of behavioral strategies in game-theory and allows the resulting logic to naturally express game-theoretic concepts. The fragment where only restricted quantifications are considered, called behavioral quantifications , can be decided, for both model checking and satisfiability , in 2 ExpTime and is expressively equivalent to Q PTL , though significantly less succinct .



I
Introduction

The tight connection between
logic
and
games
has been
acknowledged since the sixties, when first Lorenzen
[
44
]
and later
Lorenz
[
43
]
and Hintikka
[
27
]
proposed
game-theoretic
semantics
for first-order logic
[
32
,
29
]
.
In this approach, the meaning of a sentence is given in terms of a zero-sum
game played by two agents: the
verifier
, whose objective is to show the
sentence true, and the
falsifier
, with the dual objective of showing
the sentence false.
Satisfiability of a sentence, then, becomes a game between these two players
and the sentence is satisfiable (
resp.
, unsatisfiable)
iff
verifier (
resp.
,
falsifier) has a strategy to win it.
This tight connection can clearly be viewed in the other direction as well:
logic can be used to reason about games,
i.e.
, we can encode the problem of
solving a game into a
decision problem
, such as
satisfiability

or
model-checking
, of some logic.
The idea is to describe the game and the
winning condition
with a
formula of the logic and exploit the
game-theoretic interpretation
to
reduce the solution of the game to a specific decision problem for that
logic.
Essentially, the winning strategy for the game can be extracted from the
winning strategy for the
decision game
.

Suppose we have a formula
\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
, expressing a required relation
between the choice
{\mathnormal{y}}
made by a player, from now on called
Eloise
,
and a choice
{\mathnormal{x}}
made by the adversary, namely
Abelard
,
i.e.
,

\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
encodes the
objective
of a two-player game.
We say that the game is won by Eloise if there exists a
strategy
for
her such that, for each choice
{\mathnormal{x}}
made by Abelard, the corresponding
response
{\mathnormal{y}}
of Eloise using that strategy guarantees that the resulting
play satisfies the requirement
\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
.
This condition can clearly be expressed by a sentence of the form
\forall{\mathnormal{x}}\ldotp\exists{\mathnormal{y}}\ldotp\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
.
We could then solve the game by solving the satisfiability problem for
this sentence.
In other words, solving the game reduces to checking whether there exists a

Skolem function

{\mathsf{f}}
such that
\forall{\mathnormal{x}}\ldotp\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathsf{f}}({\mathnormal{x}}))
is satisfied.
This function basically dictates the response of Eloise to the choice of
Abelard, thereby encoding her strategy.

The above approach works pretty well when we consider
single-round
games
,
a.k.a.
,
normal-form games

[
68
]
, and can easily be extended
to
finite-rounds games
,
a.k.a.
,
extensive-form
games

[
67
,
40
,
41
]
, by extending the quantification prefix to a
sequence of alternations of quantifiers, one for each round.
Things, however, get much more complicated when
infinite-rounds games

come into play
[
17
,
69
]
.
For such a class of extensive-form games, indeed, plays are induced by
infinite sequences of choices made by the players over time and a strategy
dictates how a player at a given stage of a play responds to the choices
made by the adversary up to that stage.
Extending the quantification prefix to match the rounds would immediately lead
to
infinitary logics
, such as the one proposed in
[
38
]
and
further studied in
[
23
]
(see also
[
30
]
).
This technique has some interesting applications in logic
[
24
]
,
computer science
[
37
]
, and even philosophy
[
16
]
.
Besides its infinitary nature, however, this approach has also the drawback
of heavily departing from the standard Tarskian viewpoint, as only
non-compositional game-theoretic semantics has been provided.

A more viable route, instead, is to make the quantified variables
{\mathnormal{x}}
and

{\mathnormal{y}}
range over sequences of choices.
For example, if the choices are simply
Boolean values
,
i.e.
, when

iterated Boolean games
are considered
[
21
,
22
]
, first-order
extension of
temporal logics
, such as
Quantified Propositional
Temporal Logic
(
Q
PTL
)
[
63
]
, seem like a good place to start, as they
predicate over infinite sequences of temporal points, the stages of the game.
In this setting, however, the Skolem function
{\mathsf{f}}
cannot be interpreted as
a strategy in the game-theoretic sense, as its value at a given stage depends
on the entire evaluation of its argument
{\mathnormal{x}}
, namely the entire sequence
of choices made by the adversary, including all the future ones.
By contrast, a strategy for a player can only dictate, step by step, what its
responses should be, depending on the choices made so far by its opponent.
What that means is that, in principle, the satisfiability and the game
solution problems do not coincide anymore.
A classic example of this problem already appears in
[
58
]
.
Assume
\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
is the
LTL

[
56
,
57
]
formula
{\mathtt{G}}({\mathnormal{y}}\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}{\mathnormal{x}})
(or just
{\mathnormal{y}}\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}{\mathnormal{x}}
).
Clearly, the sentence
\forall{\mathnormal{x}}\ldotp\exists{\mathnormal{y}}\ldotp\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
is satisfiable.
However, there is no
“feasible”
(
i.e.
,
implementable
) strategy
that can enforce
\psi({\mathnormal{x}},{\mathnormal{y}})
, without Eloise knowing in advance the
future values that Abelard is going to choose for
{\mathnormal{x}}
in the rest of the
play.
The problem is that the standard interpretation of quantification treats the
quantified objects as
atomic entities
, regardless of their
inner
structure
, like their being sequences in the above example.
This is by no means the only exemplification of the problem, which was already
recognized in the theory of extensive-form games since its dawn
[
40
]
,
where the notion of feasible strategy, called
behavioral
, has been
introduced (see also
[
41
,
62
,
39
,
53
]
).
Another important source of unfeasible strategic behaviors is hidden in the
semantics of
Strategy Logic
(
SL
)
[
4
,
50
,
5
,
48
]
, an
extension of
LTL
that allows for explicit
quantifications
over
strategies and
binding
of strategies with players.
In this logic, formulae can be written that can be satisfied only by allowing
players to look at what other strategies dictate in the future or
counterfactual situations
[
49
]
, admitting infeasible behaviors.
Once again, the problem lies in the intrinsic dependence among the variables
quantified in the formula.

One way to reconcile quantifications and strategies in a temporal setting
would be to extend the game-theoretic interpretation of the quantifiers, and
of the logic in general, to account for the underlying temporal dynamics.
This would imply allowing the players in the satisfiability game to play with
partial information on the choices of the adversary, namely the players have
no information about the future and can only choose based on the moves played
so far in the game.
Previous attempts to address the issue typically involve resorting to
ad hoc

Skolem semantics

[
33
]
for the specific logic.
In the case of
SL
, for instance, the notion of
behavioral semantics

has been introduced
[
48
]
, which prevents the players from looking at
future choices when selecting their strategy, effectively limiting the player
observation ability to the current history in the game.
A more liberal semantics based on
timeline dependencies
has been also
proposed
[
18
,
19
]
.
While these approaches do solve the problem in the specific case, they lead to
non-compositional semantics
[
60
]
, in that the interpretation of a
formula is not defined in terms of the interpretation of its component
subformulae.
To obtain a compositional version of the game-theoretic semantics, a finer
grained technical setting is required, compared to the classic Tarskian
semantics, specifically, one that can accommodate some form of
partial
independence
among the quantified variables.

Following Tarski approach, each choice for a quantified variable in a sentence
is made with complete information about, hence it is (potentially) completely
dependent on, the values of variables quantified before it in the
sentence.
This idiosyncrasy of the classic interpretation of quantifiers is well known
and attempts have been made to overcome the
linear dependence
of
quantifiers dictated by their relative position in a
sentence
[
25
,
28
,
2
,
61
]
.
Most notably, Hintikka and Sandu
[
31
]
proposed

Independence-Friendly Logic
(
IF
), as a first-order logic where
independence between quantified variables can be explicitly asserted in the
formulae together with a game-theoretic, non-compositional,
semantics
[
59
]
for the logic.
A compositional semantics for
IF
was later proposed by
Hodges
[
34
,
35
]
, whose idea was to replace the standard notion of

assignment
of the Tarskian semantics with that of a set of assignments
(called
trump

[
34
]
or
team

[
65
]
), as the basic
semantic element with respect to which the truth of a formula is evaluated.
This multiplicity of assignments effectively allows one to express the notion of

dependence/independence
among variables, a distinction that makes very
little sense, in particular from a formal point of view, when only a single
assignment is considered.

Taking inspiration from Hodges’ work, the goal of this work is to devise a

compositional semantic framework
that can account for a game-theoretic
interpretation of quantification over (possibly infinite) sequences of
choices.
The framework is specifically tailored to deal with quantifications in a
linear-time settings and applied to the logic
Q
PTL
, which was introduced
in
[
63
]
as a unifying
\omega
-regular language
allowing for
both temporal operators and propositional quantifiers.
Despite its expressiveness and theoretical interest,
Q
PTL
has not gained much
traction in practical contexts, mainly due to the high complexity of its
decision problems.
Indeed, both the satisfiability and the model checking problems are

non-elementary
in the number of
alternations of the
quantifiers

[
64
]
.

In this article we propose a novel semantics for
Q
PTL
, inspired by the body
of work on (in)dependence logics
[
65
,
45
,
1
]
.
Similarly to those works, the semantics provides a compositional
formulation
[
60
]
for a game-theoretic interpretation of the
quantifiers.
In contrast to them, however, we require a
symmetric
treatment of the
two quantifiers in order to preserve
closure under negation
and
avoid
undetermined formulae

[
34
,
35
]
.
The most significant feature of the new approach is the ability to encode
various forms of
independence constraints
among the quantified
variables and provide a powerful tool to fine-tune the semantics of the
propositional quantifiers. In particular, we discuss a specific instantiation
of the semantics that allows one to recover a game-theoretic interpretation of
the quantifiers and reconcile the satisfiability and the game solution
problems.
This result is achieved by first generalizing classic
temporal
assignments
, which give values to propositional variables at each time
instant, to sets of sets of assignments, called
hyperassignments
.
This also generalizes teams, defined as sets of assignments, used by Hodges.
The second step is to introduce new
classes of functors
that maps
temporal assignments to valuations of a given variable over time and,
intuitively, correspond to the semantic counterparts of the Skolem functions.
The dependence of functors on assignments allows us to impose various forms of
independence constraints among the variables.
In particular, we investigate two specific forms, called
behavioral
and

strongly-behavioral
, that require functors to choose the value of the
variable at any given time instant based only upon the values dictated by the
input assignment to the other variables up to that instant (possibly
excluded).
These are forms of independence constraints that make the choice of the value
of a variable at a given time totally independent of the values that other
variables assume in the future.
The behavioral restrictions are precisely what allows us to recover the
correspondence between Skolem functions and strategies and to reconcile the
satisfiability and game solution problems, thus making the resulting version
of
Q
PTL
, called
Good-for-Games
Q
PTL

(
GFG
-
Q
PTL
), well suited to
express game-theoretic concepts and a logical analogue of
Good-for-Games
Automata

[
26
,
3
]
.

On the technical side, the novel semantics under the behavioral interpretation
of the quantifiers leads to 2
ExpTime
decision procedures for both the
satisfiability and model-checking problems.
On the other hand, it does not give up expressiveness, as we show that the
vanilla and behavioral semantics turn out to be expressively equivalent.
These results also show that the high complexity of the decision problems for
vanilla
Q
PTL
stems from the fact that unrestricted dependencies among the
quantified variables are allowed.
The properties expressible by exploiting such unrestricted dependencies can,
however, still be expressed under the behavioral semantics via encoding of

\omega
-regular automata, though with a non-elementary blowup.

II
Alternating Hodges Semantics

Q
PTL

[
63
]
extends
LTL

[
56
,
57
]
with quantifications over

atomic propositions
from a given set
{\mathrm{AP}}
, with the intuition that
the Boolean values of the same proposition in different time instants are
independent of each other.

II-A

Quantified Propositional Temporal Logic

For convenience, we provide a syntax for
Q
PTL
where quantifications do not
occur within temporal operators.
This is equivalent to the original logic, thanks to the
prenex normal
form
(
pnf
, for short) property enjoyed by
Q
PTL

[
63
]
, which allows to
move quantifiers outside temporal operators.

Definition 1

(
Q
PTL
Syntax)
.

The
Quantified Propositional Temporal Logic
is the set of formulae
built accordingly to the following context-free grammar, where
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}

and
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
:

\varphi\seteq\psi\mid\neg\varphi\mid\varphi\wedge\varphi\mid\varphi\vee\varphi\mid\exists\apElm\ldotp\varphi\mid\forall\apElm\ldotp\varphi.

The classic semantics is given in terms of
temporal assignments
(simply

assignments
, from now on), which are functions associating each
proposition with a
temporal valuation
mapping each time instant to a
Boolean value,
i.e.
, infinite sequences of truth assignments.
Let
{\mathrm{Asg}}\defeq\allowbreak{\mathrm{AP}}\pto({\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{B}})
denote the set of
assignments over arbitrary subsets of
{\mathrm{AP}}
.
For convenience, we also introduce the set of assignments defined exactly over
the propositions in
{\mathrm{P}}\!\subseteq\!{\mathrm{AP}}
,
i.e.
,
{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})\!\defeq\!{{\{\,\allowbreak\!\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Asg}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathsf{dom}\!\left(\allowbreak\asgElm\allowbreak\right)}\!=\!{\mathrm{P}}\!\allowbreak\,\}}}
and
the set
{\mathrm{Asg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathrm{P}})\!\defeq\!{{\{\,\allowbreak\!\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Asg}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathrm{P}}\!\subseteq\!{\mathsf{dom}\!\left(\allowbreak\asgElm\allowbreak\right)}\!\allowbreak\,\}}}
of assignments defined at least over

{\mathrm{P}}
.
The
satisfaction relation

\models
between an assignment
\asgElm
and a

Q
PTL
formula
\varphi
is defined below, where
{{\,\models}_{\text{LTL$$}}}
is the standard

LTL
satisfiability and
{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{f}}]
denotes the assignment
that extends
\asgElm
and maps proposition
\apElm
to temporal valuation

{\mathsf{f}}
.
As usual, by
{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}
we denote the set of propositions free in

\varphi
.

Definition 2

(Tarski Semantics)
.

The
Tarski-semantics relation

\asgElm\models\varphi
is inductively
defined as follows, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
and assignments
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{Asg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
.

1.

\asgElm\models\psi
, if
\asgElm{{\,\models}_{\text{LTL$$}}}\psi
, whenever
\psi

is an
LTL
formula;

2.

the semantics of Boolean connectives is defined as usual;

3.

for all atomic propositions
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
:

(a)

\asgElm\models\exists\apElm\ldotp\phi
if
{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{f}}]\models\phi
, for some
{\mathsf{f}}\in{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{B}}
;

(b)

\asgElm\models\forall\apElm\ldotp\phi
if
{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{f}}]\models\phi
, for all
{\mathsf{f}}\in{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{B}}
.

II-B

A New Semantics for
Q
PTL

We now introduce a novel compositional semantics for
Q
PTL
that, unlike Tarski’s
one, will allow us to specify, later on, independence constraints among
quantified propositions.
The new semantics follows an approach similar to
[
34
]
, where a
compositional semantics for
IF
was first proposed.
Hodges’ idea was to expand an assignment for the free variables to a set of
assignments, a trump in his terminology, with the intuition of capturing all
possible choices made by one of the two players for its own variables in the
satisfiability game underlying the game-theoretic semantics of the
logic
[
31
]
.
Hodges’ semantics, though able to correctly capture
IF
, is, however, not
adequate for our purposes.
Indeed, by design, it is intrinsically asymmetric, treating the two players
differently.
More specifically, a single set of assignments only provides complete
information about the choices of one of the two players and only allows to
restrict the choices of the adversary.
This, in turn, limits the class of games expressible in the logic to asymmetric
games, where only the observation power of one player can be restricted.
To also capture symmetric games, we need to get rid of this asymmetry, which
requires a non-trivial generalization of Hodges’ approach.

To give semantics to a
Q
PTL
formula
\varphi
, we proceed as follows.
Similarly to Hodges, the idea is that the interpretations of the free atomic
propositions correspond to the choices that the two players could make prior to
the current stage of the game,
i.e.
, the stage where the formula
\varphi
has
still to be evaluated.
These possible choices can be organized on a two-level structure,
i.e.
, a set of
sets of assignments, each level summarizing the information about the choices a
player can make in its turns.
In order to evaluate the formula
\varphi
, then, a player chooses a set of
assignment, while its opponent chooses one assignment in that set where

\varphi
must hold.
We shall use a flag
\alpha\in\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
, called
alternation
flag
, to keep track of which player is assigned to which level of choice.
If
\alpha={\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
, Eloise chooses the set of assignments, while Abelard chooses
one of those assignments; if
\alpha={\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
, the dual reasoning applies.
Given a flag
\alpha\in\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
, we denote by
{{\overline{\alpha}}}
the
dual flag,
i.e.
,
{{\overline{\alpha}}}\in\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
with
{{\overline{\alpha}}}\neq\alpha
.
The idea above is captured by the following notion of
hyperassignment
,
namely a non-empty set of non-trivial,
i.e.
, non-empty, sets of assignments
defined over an arbitrary set
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
:

{\mathrm{HAsg}}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq 2^{{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak\emptyset\!\not\in\!{\mathfrak{X}}\!\neq\!\emptyset\land{\mathrm{P}}\!\subseteq\!{\mathrm{AP}}\allowbreak\,\}}}.

Note that we require all the assignments contained in a hyperassignment to be
defined on the same atomic propositions, though the domains of assignments in
different hyperassignment may differ.
By
{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
we denote the set of atomic propositions over
which the hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
is defined.

{\mathrm{HAsg}}({\mathrm{P}})\defeq\allowbreak{{\{\,\allowbreak\!{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq 2^{{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})}\!\allowbreak\,\}}}
is the set of hyperassignments over the same
set of atomic propositions
{\mathrm{P}}
, while the set whose hyperassignments have
domains that include
{\mathrm{P}}
is
{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathrm{P}})\defeq\allowbreak{{\{\,\allowbreak\!{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq 2^{{\mathrm{Asg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathrm{P}})}\!\allowbreak\,\}}}
.

{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{11}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{12}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{13}}}
\asgElm[1]\;\asgElm[3]\;\asgElm[5]
\asgElm[6]\;\asgElm[7]\;\asgElm[8]
\asgElm[7]\;\asgElm[8]\;\asgElm[10]
\asgElm[2]\;\asgElm[4]
\asgElm[2]\;\asgElm[4]
\asgElm[6]\;\asgElm[9]
\sqsubseteq
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{21}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{22}}}
{\mathrm{X}_{23}}
\asgElm[2]\;\asgElm[4]
\asgElm[6]\;\asgElm[9]
\asgElm[1]\;\asgElm[9]\;\asgElm[11]

Figure 1:
The preorder between hyperassignments: for
every
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{1i}}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
, there is a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{2j}}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{2j}}}\!\subseteq\!{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{1i}}}
. More than one set in
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}

could choose the same set in
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
; there may be sets in
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}

not used by any in
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
.

For any pair of hyperassignments
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
, we write

{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
to express the fact that, for all sets of
assignments
{\mathrm{X}_{1}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
, there is a set of assignments
{\mathrm{X}_{2}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
with
{\mathrm{X}_{2}}\subseteq{\mathrm{X}_{1}}
.
Obviously,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
, which in its turn implies
{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}={\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\allowbreak\right)}
.
Figure
1
reports a graphical representation of the relation

\sqsubseteq
.
As usual, we write
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\equiv{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
if both
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
hold true.
It is clear that the relation
\sqsubseteq
is both reflexive and transitive,
hence it is a preorder.
Consequently,
\equiv
is an equivalence relation.
In particular, we shall show (see Corollary
) that
\equiv

captures the intuitive notion of equivalence between hyperassignments, in the
sense that two equivalent hyperassignments
w.r.t.

\equiv
do satisfy the same
formulae.

Our goal is to define a semantics for
Q
PTL
by providing a satisfaction relation
between a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
and a
Q
PTL
formula
\varphi
,
w.r.t.
a given
interpretation of the players of
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
i.e.
,
w.r.t.
an alternation flag

\alpha\in\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
.
Therefore, we shall have two satisfaction relations, namely
{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}

and
{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}
, depending on how we interpret the levels of the
hyperassignment.
The idea is to capture the following intuition that relates, in a natural way,
to the classic Tarskian semantics.
When the alternation flag
\alpha
is
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
, then a set of assignments is
chosen existentially by Eloise and all its assignments, chosen universally by
Abelard, must satisfy
\varphi
.
Conversely, when
\alpha
is
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
, then a set of assignments is chosen
universally by Abelard and at least one assignment, chosen existentially by
Eloise, must satisfy
\varphi
.
Semantically, hyperassignments are a notion similar to

quasi-strategies

[
38
]
.

We break down the presentation of the semantics by introducing three operations:
the
dualization
swaps the role of the two players in a hyperassignment,
allowing for connecting the two satisfaction relations and a symmetric treatment
of quantifiers later on; the
partitioning
deals with disjunction and
conjunction; finally, the
extension
directly handles quantifications.

Let us consider the dualization operator first.
The idea is that, given a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
, the dual hyperassignment

{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
exchanges the role of the two players
w.r.t.

{\mathfrak{X}}
.
This means that, if Eloise is the first to choose in
{\mathfrak{X}}
, then her choice
will be postponed in
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
after that of Abelard.
To ensure that, in exchanging the order of choice for the two players, we do not
alter the semantics of the underlying game, we need to reshuffle the assignments
in
{\mathfrak{X}}
so as to simulate the original dependencies between the choices of
the players.
To this end, we introduce the set of choice functions for
{\mathfrak{X}}
as follows,
whose definition implicitly assumes the axiom of choice:

{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}\to{\mathrm{Asg}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak\forall{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}\ldotp{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({\mathrm{X}})\in{\mathrm{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}.

{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
contains all the functions
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
that, for every set of
assignments
{\mathrm{X}}
in
{\mathfrak{X}}
, pick a specific assignment
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({\mathrm{X}})
in
that set.
Each such function simulates a possible choice of the second player of
{\mathfrak{X}}

depending on the choice of (the set of assignments chosen by) its first player.
The dual hyperassignment
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, then, collects the images of
the choice functions in
{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
.
We, thus, obtain a hyperassignment in which the choice order of the two players is inverted:

{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\,\}}}.

{\mathfrak{X}}\!=\!\begin{Bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{1}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[11]},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[12]}\},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[21]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[22]}\},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}{\mathrm{X}_{3}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}\asgElm[3]}\}\end{Bmatrix}{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\!=\!\begin{Bmatrix}{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[11]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[21]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}\asgElm[3]}\},\\
{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}}\allowbreak\right)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[11]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[22]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}\asgElm[3]}\},\\
{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{3}}\allowbreak\right)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[12]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[21]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}\asgElm[3]}\},\\
{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{4}}\allowbreak\right)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[12]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[22]},{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}\asgElm[3]}\}\phantom{,}\end{Bmatrix}

Example 1.

Consider the hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
on the right, where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{1}}}\!=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[11]},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\asgElm[12]}\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}\!=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[21]},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\asgElm[22]}\}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}{\mathrm{X}_{3}}}\!=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}\asgElm[3]}\}
.
Every set of assignments in
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
is obtained as the image of one of
the four choice functions
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{i}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
, each choosing
exactly one assignment from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathrm{X}_{1}}}
, one from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}
, and one from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.25,0}{\mathrm{X}_{3}}}
.
Intuitively, in
{\mathfrak{X}}
the strategy of the first player, say Eloise, can only
choose the color of the final assignments (either red for
\mathrm{X}_{1}
, blue for
\mathrm{X}_{2}
,
or green for
\mathrm{X}_{3}
), while the one for Abelard decides which assignment of
each color will be picked.
After dualization, the two players exchange the order in which they choose.
Therefore, Abelard, starting first in
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, will select one of the
four choice functions, which picks an assignment for each color.
Eloise, choosing second, by using her strategy that selects the color will give
the final assignment.
In other words, the original strategies of the players encoded in the
hyperassignment, as well as their dependencies, are preserved, regardless of the
swap of their role in the dual hyperassignment.

The following proposition ensures that the dualization operator enjoys an

involution property
, similarly to the Boolean negation: by applying the
dualization twice, we obtain a hyperassignment equivalent to the original one.

Proposition 1
.

{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}\equiv{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
, for all
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
.

Note that there is a clear analogy between the structure of hyperassignments
with alternation flag
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
(
resp.
,
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
) and the structure of DNF (
resp.
,
CNF) formulae, where the dualization swaps between two equivalent forms.
The following lemma formally states that the dualization swaps the role of the
two players while still preserving the original dependencies among their
choices.

Lemma 1

(Dualization)
.

The following hold true, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
and hyperassignments

{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
.

1.

Statements
and
are equivalent:

(a)

there exists
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
,
for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
,
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for
some
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

2.

Statements
and
are equivalent:

(a)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for some

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

there exists
{\mathrm{X}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

The
partition operator
decomposes hyperassignments and is instrumental in
capturing the semantics of Boolean connectives.
Given a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
, the following set

{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in 2^{{\mathfrak{X}}}\times 2^{{\mathfrak{X}}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\uplus{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}={\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}

collects all the possible partitions of
{\mathfrak{X}}
into two parts.
Assume that the two players of
{\mathfrak{X}}
are interpreted according to the
alternation flag
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
: Abelard chooses first and Eloise chooses second.
The game-theoretic interpretation of the disjunction requires Eloise to choose
one of two disjuncts to be proven true.
In our setting, then, in order to satisfy
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
, Eloise
has to show that, for each set of assignments chosen by Abelard, she has a way
to
select one of the disjuncts
\varphi_{i}
in such a way that
\varphi_{i}
is
satisfied by some assignment in that set.
This selection is summarized by one of the pairs
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})
in

{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
, where
{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
collects the sets of assignments for
which the
i
-th disjunct is selected, with
i\in\{1,2\}
.
A similar argument, with the role of the two players reversed and switching the
quantifications throughout, leads to a dual interpretation for conjunction,
where it is Abelard who chooses one of the two conjuncts to be proven false.
This intuition is made precise by the following lemma.

Lemma 2

(Boolean Connectives)
.

The following hold true, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi_{1}
and

\varphi_{2}
and hyperassignments
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathrm{P}})
,
with
{\mathrm{P}}\defeq\allowbreak{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi_{1}\allowbreak\right)}\cup{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi_{2}\allowbreak\right)}
.

1.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

there is
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\!\models\!\varphi_{1}\!\wedge\!\varphi_{2}
, for all
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

for each
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
, there are

i\in\{1,2\}
and
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi_{i}
, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

2.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\asgElm\!\models\!\varphi_{1}\!\vee\!\varphi_{2}
, for some
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
;​

(b)

there is
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\!\in\!{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such
that, for all
i\in\{1,2\}
and
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
,

\asgElm\!\models\!\varphi_{i}
, for some
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
.

Quantifications are taken care of by the
extension operator
.
Let
{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathrm{P}})\defeq{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})\to({\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{B}})
be the set of

functors
that maps every assignment over
{\mathrm{P}}
to a temporal valuation.
Essentially, these objects play the role of Skolem functions in the
non-compositional semantics.
The
extension of an assignment

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})

w.r.t.
a
functor
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathrm{P}})
for an atomic proposition
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}

is defined as
{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak\asgElm,{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\defeq{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm)]
.
Intuitively, it extends
\asgElm
with
\apElm
, by assigning to it the value

{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm)
prescribed by the functor
{\mathsf{F}}
.
The
extension operation
can then be lifted to sets of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\!\subseteq\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
in the obvious way,
i.e.
, we set
{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak\asgElm,{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}
.
This operation embeds into
{\mathrm{X}}
the entire player strategy encoded by

{\mathsf{F}}
.
Finally, the
extension of a hyperassignment

{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}({\mathrm{P}})

with
\apElm
is simply the set of extensions with
\apElm
of all its sets of
assignments
w.r.t.
all possible functors over the atomic propositions of

{\mathfrak{X}}
:

{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}},{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)})\allowbreak\,\}}}.

Intuitively, this operation embeds into
{\mathfrak{X}}
all possible strategies,
encoded by the functors
{\mathsf{F}}
, for choosing the value of
\apElm
.
The following lemma states that the extension operator provides an adequate
semantics for quantifications, where statement
considers
Eloise’s choices, when the player interpretation of the hyperassignment is

{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
, and statement
takes care of Abelard’s choices, when
the player interpretation is
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
.

Lemma 3

(Hyperassignment Extensions)
.

The following hold true, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
, atomic propositions

\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
, and hyperassignments
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}\setminus\{\apElm\})
.

1.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

there is
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\models\exists\apElm\ldotp\varphi
, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

there is
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
such that
\asgElm\!\!\models\!\!\varphi
, for all
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
.

2.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\asgElm\models\forall\apElm\ldotp\varphi
, for some
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
,
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for some
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

We can finally introduce the new semantics for
Q
PTL
based on the novel notion
of hyperassignment.

Definition 3

(Alternating Hodges Semantics)
.

The
alternating-Hodges-semantics relation

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
is
defined as follows, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
, hyperassignments

{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
, and alternation flags

\alpha\in\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
.

1.

whenever
\psi
is an
LTL
formula:

(a)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\psi
if there is a set of assignments

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that, for each assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
, it holds that
\asgElm{{\,\models}_{\text{LTL$$}}}\>\psi
;

(b)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\psi
if, for each set of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, there is an assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that

\asgElm{{\,\models}_{\text{LTL$$}}}\>\psi
;

2.

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\neg\phi
if
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\not\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\phi
,
i.e.
, it is not the case that

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\phi
;

3.

(a)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}
if, for each

({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
, it holds that

{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi_{1}

or
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi_{2}
;

(b)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}
if
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}
;

5.

for all atomic propositions
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
:

(a)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\exists\apElm\ldotp\phi
if

{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi
;

(b)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\exists\apElm\ldotp\phi
if

{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\exists\apElm\ldotp\phi
;

4.

(a)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2}
if there is

({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such that if

{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!\neq\!\emptyset
then
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi_{1}
and if
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!\neq\!\emptyset
then
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi_{2}
;

(b)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2}
if
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2}
;

6.

for all atomic propositions
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
:

(a)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\forall\apElm\ldotp\phi
if

{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\forall\apElm\ldotp\phi
;

(b)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\forall\apElm\ldotp\phi
if

{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi
.

The base case (Item
1
) for
LTL
formulae
\psi
simply
formalizes the intuition about satisfaction relative to the alternation flag: if

\alpha\!=\!{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
, there exists a set of assignments whose elements satisfy

\psi
in the Tarski sense; the dual applies when
\alpha\!=\!{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
.
Negation, in accordance with the classic game-theoretic interpretation, is dealt
with by simply exchanging the player interpretation of the hyperassignment
(Item
2
).
Observe that, from this semantic condition, it immediately follows that either

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi
or
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\neg\varphi
.
In other words, the semantics does not allow formulae with an undetermined truth
value.
The semantics of the remaining Boolean connectives
(Items
3a
and
4a
) and
quantifiers (Items
5a
and
6b
)
is a direct application of Lemmata
and
.
Observe that swapping between
{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}
and
{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}

(Items
3b
,
4b
,

5b
and
6a
) is done according
to Lemma
and it represents the fundamental point where our
approach departs from Hodges’ semantics
[
34
,
35
]
.
The above three lemmata also imply the following theorem, which formalizes an

adequacy principle
that reduces the two satisfiability relations of the
new semantics to the classic Tarskian satisfaction in a natural way.

Theorem 1

(Semantics Adequacy I)
.

For all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
and hyperassignments
\!{\mathfrak{X}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
:

1.

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\varphi

iff
there exists a set of
assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for
all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

2.

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\varphi

iff
, for all sets of assignments

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, it holds that
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for some

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

Example 2
.

Let us consider the
Q
PTL
sentence
\varphi\defeq\forall\apElm\ldotp(\psi_{\apElm}\rightarrow\exists\qapElm\ldotp(\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm)))
, with
\psi_{\apElm}\defeq\neg\apElm\wedge{\mathtt{X}}({\mathtt{G}}\apElm\vee{\mathtt{G}}\neg\apElm)
and
\psi_{\qapElm}\defeq{\mathtt{G}}\qapElm\vee{\mathtt{G}}\neg\qapElm
.
It can be viewed as describing a very simple game with two players, Abelard
and Eloise in this order.
Abelard can only choose a truth value for
\apElm
that will hold constant at
any time instant except for time
0
, where it is false regardless of his
choice, in accordance with
\psi_{\apElm}
.
Eloise, instead, chooses a truth value for
\qapElm
that will hold constant
from time
0
onward, as dictated by
\psi_{\qapElm}
.
The
LTL
formula
\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm
encodes the game
objective, requiring that the truth value of
\apElm
at time
1
matches that
of
\qapElm
at time
0
.
Sentence
\varphi
, then, asks whether Eloise can respond with one of her
legal moves to every legal move by Abelard so that the objective is always
met.
Let us now apply the semantic rules given in Definition
3
,
from which we derive the following deduction steps.

1.

\{\{{{\varnothing}}\}\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\varphi
;

2.

\{\{\asgElm[\apElm]\},\{\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\},\ldots\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\psi_{\apElm}\rightarrow\exists\qapElm\ldotp(\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm))
;

3.

\{\ldots\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\neg\psi_{\apElm}
and

\{\{\asgElm[\apElm]\},\{\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\}\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\exists\qapElm\ldotp(\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm))
;

4.

\{\{\asgElm[\apElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\}\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\exists\qapElm\ldotp(\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm))
;

5.

\{\{\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}\qapElm]\},\{\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\},\{\asgElm[\apElm{{\overline{\qapElm}}}],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}\qapElm]\},\{\asgElm[\apElm{{\overline{\qapElm}}}],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\},\ldots\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm)
.

Being
\varphi
a sentence, it is satisfiable
iff
Step
1

holds true.
By Rule
6b
of Definition
3
on
universal quantifications, we derive Step
2
, where

\asgElm[\apElm]\defeq\{\apElm\mapsto\bot\top^{\omega}\}
and

\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\defeq\{\apElm\mapsto\bot^{\omega}\}
are the
only two assignments satisfying the precondition
\psi_{\apElm}
.
The first assignment is obtained by extending
{{\varnothing}}
by means of the
constant functor
{\mathsf{F}_{\!\bot\!\top}}
which returns false at time
0
and true at
every future instant,
i.e.
,
\asgElm[\apElm]={\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\varnothing}},{\mathsf{F}_{\!\bot\!\top}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
.
Similarly, the second one is obtained by the constant functor

{\mathsf{F}_{\bot}}
returning false at any time.
The assignments obtained by the uncountably many remaining functors are
summarized by the ellipsis.
Now, due to the semantics of disjunction, see
Rule
4a
, we can split the hyperassignment into two
parts:
\{\ldots\}
containing all the singleton sets of those assignments
violating
\psi_{\apElm}
and its complement
\{\{\asgElm[\apElm]\},\{\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\}\}
.
On the first hyperassignment we need to check
\neg\psi_{\apElm}
, while on
the second one the remaining part of the formula, as stated in
Step
3
.
Since
\{\ldots\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\neg\psi_{\apElm}
holds by
construction, Rule
5b
applied to the second part
leads to Step
4
, where we use the equality
\{\{\asgElm[\apElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\}\}={{\overline{\{\{\asgElm[\apElm]\},\{\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\}\}}}}
.
Rule
5a
on existential quantifications allows, then,
to derive Step
5
, where
\asgElm[\flat\qapElm]\defeq{\asgElm[\flat]}[\qapElm\mapsto\top^{\omega}]
and

\asgElm[\flat{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\defeq{\asgElm[\flat]}[\qapElm\mapsto\bot^{\omega}]
, with
\flat\in\{\apElm,{{\overline{\apElm}}}\}
.
The relevant sets of assignments in the hyperassignment at
Step
5
are obtained as follows:

(a)

\{\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}\qapElm]\}={\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak\{\asgElm[\apElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\},{\mathsf{F}_{\top}},\qapElm\allowbreak\right)}
, where
{\mathsf{F}_{\top}}
is the constant functor returning true at
every time;

(b)

\{\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\}={\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak\{\asgElm[\apElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\},{\mathsf{F}_{\apElm}},\qapElm\allowbreak\right)}
, where
{\mathsf{F}_{\apElm}}(\asgElm)
returns at time
i
the value of

\apElm
in
\asgElm
at
i+1
;

(c)

\{\asgElm[\apElm{{\overline{\qapElm}}}],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}\qapElm]\}={\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak\{\asgElm[\apElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\},{\mathsf{F}_{{{\overline{\apElm}}}}},\qapElm\allowbreak\right)}
, where
{\mathsf{F}_{{{\overline{\apElm}}}}}(\asgElm)

returns at time
i
the dual value of
\apElm
in
\asgElm
at
i+1
;

(d)

\{\asgElm[\apElm{{\overline{\qapElm}}}],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\}={\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak\{\asgElm[\apElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}]\},{\mathsf{F}_{\bot}},\qapElm\allowbreak\right)}
, where
{\mathsf{F}_{\bot}}
is, as above, the constant functor returning false at
every time.

At this point, since
\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm)
is an
LTL
formula, Rule
1
of
Definition
3
can be applied, thus asking for a set of
assignments containing only assignments that make
\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm)
true.
Both assignments in the doubleton
\{\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\}
satisfy the
LTL
formula

\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm)
, which implies
that
\varphi
is satisfiable, witnessing Eloise’s win.

III
Good-for-Game
Q
PTL

The semantic framework introduced in the previous section allows us to encode
behavioral independence constraints among the quantified variables of
Q
PTL
.
We thus obtain the logic
GFG
-
Q
PTL
, an extension of
Q
PTL
able to express the
behavioralness of quantifications over temporal valuations.

III-A

Adding Behavioral Dependencies to
Q
PTL

Given a set of assignments
{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
over some
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
,
a
behavioral quantification

w.r.t.
a proposition
\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}}
should
choose, for each assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
, a temporal valuation

{\mathsf{f}}\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{B}}
in such a way that, intuitively, at each instant
of time
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the value
{\mathsf{f}}(k)
of
{\mathsf{f}}
at
k
only depends on
the values
\asgElm(\apElm)(t)
of the temporal valuation
\asgElm(\apElm)
at
the instants of time
t\leq k
; this means that
{\mathsf{f}}(k)
is independent of
the values
\asgElm(\apElm)(t)
at any future instant
t>k
.
To be more precise, consider two assignments
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
that may differ only on
\apElm
strictly after
k
.
Then, the functor
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathrm{P}})
interpreting a quantification
behavioral
w.r.t.

\apElm
must return the same value at
k
as a reply to both

\asgElm[1]
and
\asgElm[2]
,
i.e.
,
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(k)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(k)
; in other words,
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm)(k)
cannot exploit the
knowledge of the values
\asgElm(\apElm)(t)
, with
t>k
.
An analogous concept has been introduced in
SL

[
48
]
.
A stronger notion of behavioralness, similar to one reported in
[
18
]
,
requires the functor
{\mathsf{F}}
to satisfy the above equality when
\asgElm[1]
and

\asgElm[2]
only (possibly) differ on
\apElm
for
t\!\geq\!k
and leads to the
concept of
strongly behavioral quantification
.
In game-theoretic terms, the interpretation of a
behavioral quantifier

w.r.t.

\apElm
requires the corresponding player to
choose the value of a proposition at each round only based on the choices for

\apElm
made by the adversary up to that round.
For a
strongly behavioral quantifier
, instead, the adversary keeps its
choice for
\apElm
at the current round hidden and the player can only access
the choices made for
\apElm
at previous rounds.
Definitions
4
and
5
formalize these fundamental
concepts.

Definition 4

(Assignment Distinguishability)
.

Let
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
be two assignments over some
set
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
of propositions,
\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}}
one of these
propositions, and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
a number.
Then,
\asgElm[1]
and
\asgElm[2]
are
(\apElm,k)
-strict
distinguishable
(
resp.
,
(\apElm,k)
-distinguishable
), in symbols

\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{>k}\!\asgElm[2]
(
resp.
,
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq k}\!\asgElm[2]
), if the following holds:

1.

\asgElm[1](\qapElm)=\asgElm[2](\qapElm)
, for all
\qapElm\in{\mathrm{P}}

with
\qapElm\neq\apElm
;

2.

\asgElm[1](\apElm)(t)=\asgElm[2](\apElm)(t)
, for all
t\leq k

(
resp.
,
t<k
).

The notion of
(\apElm,k)
-strict distinguishability (
resp.
,
(\apElm,k)
-distinguishability) allows us to identify all the assignments that can only
differ on the proposition
\apElm
at some instant
t\!>\!k
(
resp.
,
t\!\geq\!k
).
Indeed,
\approx_{\apElm}^{>k}
(
resp.
,
\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq k}
) is an
equivalence relation on
{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
, whose equivalence classes identify
those assignments precisely.
A
behavioral
(
resp.
,
strongly-behavioral
) functor must reply at
time
k
uniformly to all
\approx_{\apElm}^{>k}
-equivalent (
resp.
,

\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq k}
-equivalent) assignments.

\begin{matrix}&0&1&2&3&4&5&\\
\asgElm[1]=\{\;\apElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\;\}\\
\asgElm[2]=\{\;\apElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&\cdots\;\}\\[1.00006pt]
\hline\cr\\[-8.00003pt]
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}}(\asgElm[1])=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&\cdots\\
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}}(\asgElm[2])=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\\[1.00006pt]
\hline\cr\\[-8.00003pt]
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}(\asgElm[1])=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&\cdots\\
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}(\asgElm[2])=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\\[1.00006pt]
\hline\cr\\[-8.00003pt]
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}(\asgElm[1])=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&\cdots\\
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}(\asgElm[2])=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\end{matrix}

Figure 2:
Two
\approx_{\apElm}^{>3}
(
resp.
,

\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq 4}
)-equivalent assignments and some functors.

Definition 5

(Behavioral Functor)
.

Let
{\mathsf{F}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathrm{P}})
be a functor over some set
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
of propositions and
\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}}
one of these propositions.
Then,
{\mathsf{F}}
is
behavioral
(
resp.
,
strongly behavioral
)
w.r.t.

\apElm
if
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(k)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(k)
, for all numbers
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and pairs of
\approx_{\apElm}^{>k}
-equivalent (
resp.
,

\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq k}
-equivalent) assignments
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
.

Example 3
.

Let
\asgElm[1]
and
\asgElm[2]
be two assignments over the singleton
\{\apElm\}
defined as in Figure
2
.
It is clear that
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{>3}\!\asgElm[2]
, but

\asgElm[1]\!\not\approx_{\apElm}^{>4}\!\asgElm[2]
, and so
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq 4}\!\asgElm[2]
, but
\asgElm[1]\!\not\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq 5}\!\asgElm[2]
.
Also, consider the three functors
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}},{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{B}}}},{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}(\{\apElm\})
defined as follows, for all
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{Asg}}(\{\apElm\})
and
t\!\in\!{\mathbb{N}}
:

{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}}(\asgElm)(t)\defeq\asgElm(\apElm)(t+1)
;

{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}(\asgElm)(t)\defeq{{\overline{\asgElm(\apElm)(t)}}}
;

{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}(\asgElm)(t)\defeq{\mathtt{\top}}
, if
t=0
, and

{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}(\asgElm)(t)\defeq\asgElm(\apElm)(t-1)
, otherwise.
It is immediate to see that
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
is behavioral, while
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}

is strongly behavioral.
However,
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}}
does not enjoy any behavioral property, being defined
as a future-dependent functor.
Indeed,
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}}(\asgElm[1])(3)\neq{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{A}}}}(\asgElm[2])(3)
, even
though
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{>3}\!\asgElm[2]
.

To capture in the logic the behavioral constraints on the functors, we extend

Q
PTL
with additional decorations for the quantifiers that express behavioral
dependencies among the propositions involved.
The result is a new logic, called
Good-for-Games
Q
PTL

, able to express
in a natural way game-theoretic concepts of Boolean games.

Definition 6

(
GFG
-
Q
PTL
Syntax)
.

Good-for-Games
Q
PTL

(
GFG
-
Q
PTL

) is the set of formulae built
accordingly to the
following context-free grammar, where
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}
,
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
,
and
{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}},{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
:

\displaystyle\varphi\seteq\psi\mid\neg\varphi\mid\varphi\wedge\varphi\mid\varphi\vee\varphi\mid\exists\apElm\colon\!\spcElm\ldotp\varphi\mid\forall\apElm\colon\!\spcElm\ldotp\varphi;

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\spcElm\seteq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym] }{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym] }.

A propositional quantifier of the form
{\mathtt{Q}}\apElm\colon\!\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym]
}{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym] }
explicitly expresses a
{\mathtt{Q}}
-quantification over
\apElm
,

i.e.
, a choice of a functor to interpret
\apElm
, that is behavioral
w.r.t.
all
the propositions in
{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
and strongly-behavioral
w.r.t.
those in

{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
.

To ease the notation, we may write
{\mathtt{Q}^{\spcElm}}\apElm\ldotp\varphi

instead of
{\mathtt{Q}}\apElm\colon\!\spcElm\ldotp\varphi
, write

\left<{\mathtt{B}}:{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\right>
and
\left<{\mathtt{S}}:{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}\right>
for
\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym] }{\SSym:
\emptyset}
and
\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \emptyset}{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym] }
, respectively, and
{\mathtt{B}}

and
{\mathtt{S}}
instead of
\left<{\mathtt{B}}:{\mathrm{AP}}\right>
and
\left<{\mathtt{S}}:{\mathrm{AP}}\right>
.
We also omit the quantifier specification
\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \emptyset}{\SSym: \emptyset}
,
using
{\mathtt{Q}}\apElm\ldotp\varphi
to denote
{\mathtt{Q}}\apElm\colon\!\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym:
\emptyset}{\SSym: \emptyset}\ldotp\varphi
.
Finally, we may drop the curly bracket for the sets
{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
and

{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
and write
\left<{\mathtt{B}}:\apElm,\qapElm\right>
instead of
\left<{\mathtt{B}}:\{\apElm,\qapElm\}\right>
.

We say that a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula is
behavioral
(
resp.
,

strongly-behavioral
) if it is in prenex form and all its quantifier
specifications are equal to
{\mathtt{B}}
(
resp.
,
{\mathtt{S}}
).
We denote by
{\mathrm{Qn}}
(
resp.
,
{\mathrm{Qn}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
) the set of (
resp.
,
behavioral) quantifier prefixes and by
{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
the set of quantifier
specifications.

Given assignments
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\!\in\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
, we write

\asgElm[1]\!\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\!\asgElm[2]
, for some
\spcElm=\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym:
\PSet[\BSym] }{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym] }\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if one of the
following conditions holds:

1.

\asgElm[1]=\asgElm[2]
;

2.

\asgElm[1]\approx_{\apElm}^{>k}\asgElm[2]
, for some
\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
;

3.

\asgElm[1]\approx_{\apElm}^{\geq k}\asgElm[2]
, for some
\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
.

We denote by
\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}
the transitive closure of the reflexive
and symmetric relation
\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}
.

Proposition 2
.

Let
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
,
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
,

\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
\asgElm[1]\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[2]

iff
the following hold
true:

1.

\asgElm[1](\qapElm)=\asgElm[2](\qapElm)
, for all
\qapElm\in{\mathrm{P}}\setminus({\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\cup{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}})
;

2.

\asgElm[1](\apElm)(t)=\asgElm[2](\apElm)(t)
, for all
t\leq k
and

\apElm\in({\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\cap{\mathrm{P}})\setminus{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
;

3.

\asgElm[1](\apElm)(t)=\asgElm[2](\apElm)(t)
, for all
t<k
and

\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}\cap{\mathrm{P}}
.

Example 4
.

Consider the three assignments
\asgElm[1]
,
\asgElm[2]
, and
\asgElm[3]

over the doubleton
\{\apElm,\qapElm\}
depicted in
Figure
3
.
It is easy to see that
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{>3}\!\asgElm[2]
and

\asgElm[2]\!\approx_{\qapElm}^{\geq 3}\!\asgElm[3]
.
Therefore,
\asgElm[1]\!\sim_{\spcElm}^{3}\!\asgElm[2]\!\sim_{\spcElm}^{3}\!\asgElm[3]
, where
\spcElm\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \apElm}{\SSym:
\qapElm}
, which implies
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\spcElm}^{3}\!\asgElm[3]
.

\begin{matrix}&&0&1&2&3&4&5&\\
\hbox{\multirowsetup\asgElm[1] = \Big\lx@text@lbrace}&\apElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots&\hbox{\multirowsetup\Big\lx@text@rbrace}\\
&\qapElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\\
\hbox{\multirowsetup\asgElm[2] = \Big\lx@text@lbrace}&\apElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&\cdots&\hbox{\multirowsetup\Big\lx@text@rbrace}\\
&\qapElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\\
\hbox{\multirowsetup\asgElm[3] = \Big\lx@text@lbrace}&\apElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&\cdots&\hbox{\multirowsetup\Big\lx@text@rbrace}\\
&\qapElm\colon&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\bot}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.35,0}{\mathtt{\top}}}&\cdots\end{matrix}

Figure 3:
Three
\approx_{\spcElm}^{3}
-equivalent
assignments, with
\spcElm\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \apElm}{\SSym: \qapElm}
.

Given a set of propositions
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
and a quantifier
specification
\spcElm\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym] }{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym] }\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
,
we introduce the set of
\spcElm
-functors

{\mathrm{Fnc}_{\spcElm}}({\mathrm{P}})\subseteq{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathrm{P}})
containing exactly those
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathrm{P}})

that are behavioral
w.r.t.
all the propositions in
{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\cap{\mathrm{P}}
and
strongly behavioral
w.r.t.
those in
{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}\cap{\mathrm{P}}
.

Example 5
.

Any
\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \apElm}{\SSym: \qapElm}
-functor
{\mathsf{F}}
replies to all assignments of
Figure
3
uniformly, for all time instants between
0
and
3

included.
Indeed,
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(3)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(3)
, since
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\apElm}^{>3}\!\asgElm[2]
, being
{\mathsf{F}}
behavioral
w.r.t.

\apElm
.
Similarly,
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(3)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[3])(3)
, since
\asgElm[2]\!\approx_{\qapElm}^{\geq 3}\!\asgElm[3]
, being
{\mathsf{F}}
strongly-behavioral

w.r.t.

\qapElm
.
Hence,
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(3)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[3])(3)
.

The following proposition ensures that the above observation highlights a
general phenomenon.

Proposition 3
.

If
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\!\asgElm[2]
then

{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(k)\!=\!{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(k)
, for all
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\!\in\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
,
\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
,
k\!\in\!{\mathbb{N}}
,
and
{\mathsf{F}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{Fnc}_{\spcElm}}({\mathrm{P}})
.

We can now extend to
GFG
-
Q
PTL
the alternating Hodges semantics of
Q
PTL
reported in Definition
3
.
We simply need to parameterize the extension operation for hyperassignments with
the corresponding specification of the behavioral dependencies:

{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}},{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}_{\spcElm}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)})\allowbreak\,\}}}.

Definition 7

(Alternating Hodges Semantics Revisited)
.

The alternating-Hodges-semantics relation
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi

is inductively defined as in Definition
3
, for all but
Items
5a
and
6b
that are
modified, respectively, as follows, for all propositions
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}

and quantifier specifications
\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
:

5a
’)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\exists\apElm\colon\!\spcElm\ldotp\phi

if
{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi
;

6b
’)

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\forall\apElm\colon\!\spcElm\ldotp\phi

if
{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi
.

Notice that one could easily extend both the syntax and semantics of the
quantifier specification
\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym] }{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym] }
of
GFG
-
Q
PTL
in
order to accommodate other types of (in)dependence constraints, like the ones
already studied in first-order logic of incomplete
information
[
31
,
34
,
65
,
45
,
20
]
.
It would suffice to introduce suitable class of functors and corresponding
construct, such as the
dependence atoms
of dependence logic, whose
semantics can be easily defined via hyperassignments.

For every
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula
\varphi
and alternation flag
\alpha\in\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
, we say that
\varphi
is
\alpha
-satisfiable
if there exists a
hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{HAsg}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
such that
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi
.
Also,
\varphi

\alpha
-implies
(
resp.
, is
\alpha
-equivalent

to) a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula
\phi
, in symbols
\!\varphi{{\,\Rightarrow}^{\alpha}}\phi\!
(
resp.
,
\!\varphi{{\,\equiv}^{\alpha}}\phi
), whenever
{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}\!=\!{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\phi\allowbreak\right)}
and if
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi
then
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\phi
(
resp.
,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi

iff

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\phi
), for all
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
.
Finally, we say that
\varphi
is
satisfiable
if it is both
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
- and

{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
-satisfiable, and write
\varphi\Rightarrow\phi
(
resp.
,
\varphi\equiv\phi
) if both
\varphi{{\,\Rightarrow}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi
and
\varphi{{\,\Rightarrow}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi
(
resp.
,
\varphi{{\,\equiv}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi
and
\varphi{{\,\equiv}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi
)
hold.

At this point, let us consider some examples to provide some insight on the
expressive power of the new logic.

Example 6
.

Let us consider again the
Q
PTL
formula
\varphi
of Example
2
.
Obviously,
\varphi
is not realizable, as the functor
{\mathsf{F}_{\apElm}}

required to obtain the two satisfying assignments
\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm]

and
\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]
is non-behavioral, thus, not
implementable by any real transducer.
By replacing the two quantifiers with their behavioral counterparts, the
resulting
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula
\varphi_{{\mathtt{B}}}\defeq\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp(\psi_{\apElm}\rightarrow\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp(\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm))))
, is not
satisfiable anymore.
Indeed, the only behavioral functors that allow to satisfy
\psi_{\qapElm}

are
{\mathsf{F}_{\top}}
and
{\mathsf{F}_{\bot}}
and, therefore, deduction steps analogous
to the ones of Example
2
applied to
\varphi_{{\mathtt{B}}}
would lead
to
\{\{\asgElm[\apElm\qapElm],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}\qapElm]\},\{\asgElm[\apElm{{\overline{\qapElm}}}],\asgElm[{{\overline{\apElm}}}{{\overline{\qapElm}}}]\}\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\psi_{\qapElm}\wedge(\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm)
.
However, each of the two sets of assignments contains a non satisfying
assignment for the
LTL
formula
\qapElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\apElm
.

The previous example shows a satisfiable
Q
PTL
sentence whose behavioral
counterpart becomes unsatisfiable. The opposite may also occur, as the following
example illustrates.

Example 7
.

Consider the
Q
PTL
sentence
\exists\qapElm\ldotp\forall\apElm\ldotp\psi
, with
\psi\defeq\apElm\leftrightarrow{\mathtt{X}}\qapElm
.
The sentence is unsatisfiable: Abelard can falsify
\psi
by looking at the
value of
\qapElm
one instant in the future and choosing the opposite value
as the present value for
\apElm
.
However, the two
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentences
\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{S}}}\qapElm\ldotp\psi
and
\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\psi
are both satisfiable.
For the first one, it is enough to observe that the strongly-behavioral
functor
{\mathsf{F}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
of Example
3
allows to mimic any
temporal valuation assigned to the proposition
\apElm
one-instant in the
past, as required by the
LTL
property
\psi
.
For the second one, we need to show that,
{\mathsf{ext}_{{\mathtt{B}}}\!\left(\allowbreak\{{\mathrm{Y}}\},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\psi
, with
{\mathrm{Y}}={\mathrm{Asg}}(\{\qapElm\})
.
Now, let
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathsf{ext}_{{\mathtt{B}}}\!\left(\allowbreak\{{\mathrm{Y}}\},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
be an arbitrary set
of assignments obtained by extending those in
{\mathrm{Y}}
as prescribed by the
specification
{\mathtt{B}}
.
Also, consider
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\in{\mathrm{X}}
as two of those assignments
that differs on
\qapElm
at time
1
, but are equal at time
0
,
i.e.
,

\asgElm[1](\qapElm)(0)=\asgElm[2](\qapElm)(0)
, but
\asgElm[1](\qapElm)(1)\neq\asgElm[2](\qapElm)(1)
.
Due to the required behavioralness
w.r.t.

\qapElm
of the functors used in the
extension of
{\mathrm{Y}}
, we necessarily have that
\asgElm[1](\apElm)(0)=\asgElm[2](\apElm)(0)
.
As a consequence, either one between
\asgElm[1]
and
\asgElm[2]
satisfies

\psi
, as required by Item
1b
of the semantics.
In other words, Abelard is no longer allowed to look at the value of
\qapElm

in the future.
Note that
\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\psi

could not be expressed with an asymmetric Hodges-like semantics, as it cannot
restrict the universal quantifiers.

Example 8
.

Information leaks via quantification of unused variables is a well-known
phenomenon in
IF

[
65
]
.
The same occurs in
GFG
-
Q
PTL
, as the (in)equivalences below show:

\forall\apElm\ldotp\exists\uapElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\phi\equiv\forall\apElm\ldotp\exists\qapElm\ldotp\phi\not\equiv\forall\apElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\phi\equiv\forall\apElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\uapElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\phi;

where
\apElm,\qapElm\!\in\!{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\phi\allowbreak\right)}
, but
\uapElm\!\not\in\!{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\phi\allowbreak\right)}
.
Indeed, an arbitrary functor
{\mathsf{G}_{\qapElm}}
for
\qapElm
in
\forall\apElm\ldotp\exists\qapElm\ldotp\phi
can be simulated in
\forall\apElm\ldotp\exists\uapElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\phi
by the functors

{\mathsf{F}_{\uapElm}}={\mathsf{G}_{\qapElm}}
, for
\uapElm
, and
{\mathsf{F}_{\qapElm}}(\asgElm)=\asgElm(\uapElm)
, for
\qapElm
.
Clearly,
{\mathsf{F}_{\qapElm}}
, being the identity on
\uapElm
, is behavioral.
Intuitively, the unused non-behaviorally-quantified proposition

\uapElm
leaks information about the future of
\apElm
to
\qapElm
even if
the latter is behaviorally quantified, as it can see the future of

\apElm
through the present of
\uapElm
.
This does not happen, however, if
\uapElm
is forced to be behavioral (
resp.
,
strongly-behavioral).
Indeed, the behavioral (
resp.
, strongly-behavioral) fragments of
GFG
-
Q
PTL
enjoys the classic property of elimination of unused propositions.

The following example expands on the connection between
GFG
-
Q
PTL
and

GFG
-Automata briefly mentioned in the introduction and shows that
GFG
-
Q
PTL
can express the property of being
good-for-game for an automaton.

Example 9
.

It is well known that
Q
PTL
is able to express any
\omega
-regular
language
[
63
]
.
This can be proved by encoding the existence of an accepting run of an
arbitrary nondeterministic Büchi word automaton
{\mathcal{N}}
via a formula

\varphi\defeq\exists\sapElm[1]\ldots\exists\sapElm[k]\ldotp\psi
,
where
{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}=\{\apElm[1],\ldots,\apElm[n]\}
is the set of
propositions of the recognized language,
\sapElm[1],\ldots,\sapElm[k]
are
mutually exclusive fresh propositions representing the
k
states of

{\mathcal{N}}
, and
\psi
is the
LTL
formula encoding the transition function
and the acceptance condition.
Via the behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula
\varphi_{{\mathtt{B}}}\defeq\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\sapElm[1]\ldots\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\sapElm[k]\ldotp\psi
we can identify
precisely the sublanguages recognized by
{\mathcal{N}}
when the nondeterminism
is resolved in a
good-for-game
manner
[
26
]
,
i.e.
, when the choice
of a successor state is based on the prefix of the input words read up to that
moment.
Thus, the
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentence
\forall\apElm[1]\ldots\forall\apElm[n]\ldotp\allowbreak(\varphi\leftrightarrow\varphi_{{\mathtt{B}}})
is satisfiable

iff

{\mathcal{N}}
is a good-for-game automaton.

III-B

Model-Theoretic Analysis

Let us proceed with an elementary
model-theoretic analysis
of
GFG
-
Q
PTL
,
showing that it enjoys several basic properties, like
De Morgan laws
, one
would expect from a classic logic.

We start by observing the
monotonicity
of both the dualization and
extension operators
w.r.t.
the preorder
\sqsubseteq
, a simple property that is a
key tool in all subsequent statements.

Proposition 4
.

Let
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
,
\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
, and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
with
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
:

1.

{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}}}}\sqsubseteq{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}}
;

2.

{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
.

The preorder
\sqsubseteq
between hyperassignments captures the intuitive
notion of satisfaction strength
w.r.t.

GFG
-
Q
PTL
formulae.
Indeed, thanks to Item
1
of
Definition
3
, it holds that, if
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
, the hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
satisfies
w.r.t.
the
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
(
resp.
,

{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
) semantics less (
resp.
, more)
LTL
formulae than the hyperassignment

{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
,
i.e.
, if
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
(
resp.
,
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
) satisfies
\psi
, then

{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
(
resp.
,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
) does as well.
This property can easily be lifted to arbitrary
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formulae, by a
standard structural induction using the monotonicity of the dualization and
extension operators.

Theorem 2

(Hyperassignment Refinement)
.

Let
\varphi
be a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
with
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
.
Then,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\!\varphi
implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\!\varphi
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\!\varphi

implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\!\varphi
.

As an immediate consequence, we obtain the following result.​

Corollary 1

(Hyperassignment Equivalence)
.

Let
\varphi
be a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
with
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\equiv{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
.
Then,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi

iff

{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi
.

A fundamental feature of the proposed alternating semantics is the

duality
between swapping the players of a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
,
i.e.
,
swapping the alternation flag, and swapping the choices of the players,
i.e.
,
dualizing
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Indeed, the following results states that dualizing both the alternation flag

\alpha
and the hyperassignment preserves the truth of any formula.
This also implies, as one might expect, that double dualization has no effect
either.
The latter fact is also a consequence of the previous corollary, since
{\mathfrak{X}}\equiv{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
, due to Proposition
.

Theorem 3

(Double Dualization)
.

Let
\varphi
be a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula and
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
.
Then,
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\varphi

iff
