{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi

iff

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi
.

The duality property also grants that formulae satisfaction and equivalence do
not depend on the specific interpretation
\alpha
of hyperassignments: a
positive answer for
\alpha
implies the same for
{{\overline{\alpha}}}
.
This
invariance
corresponds to the intuition that Eloise and Abelard both
agree on the true and false formulae.
Similarly, if
\varphi
is considered to be equivalent to, or to imply, some other
property
\phi
by Eloise, the same equivalence, or implication, holds for
Abelard as well, and
vice versa
.

Corollary 2

(Interpretation Invariance)
.

Let
\varphi
and
\phi
be
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formulae.

\varphi
is
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
-satisfiable
iff

\varphi
is
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
-satisfiable.
Also,
\varphi{{\,\Rightarrow}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi

iff

\varphi{{\,\Rightarrow}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi

and
\varphi{{\,\equiv}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\phi

iff

\varphi{{\,\equiv}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\phi
.

Thanks to this invariance, the following Boolean laws hold.

Lemma 4

(Boolean Laws)
.

Let
\varphi
,
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
be
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formulae:

1.

\varphi\equiv\neg\neg\varphi
;

2.

\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\Rightarrow\varphi_{1}
;

3.

\varphi_{1}\Rightarrow\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
;

4.

\varphi_{1}\!\wedge\!(\varphi\!\wedge\!\varphi_{2})\equiv(\varphi_{1}\!\wedge\!\varphi)\!\wedge\!\varphi_{2}
;

5.

\varphi_{1}\!\vee\!(\varphi\!\vee\!\varphi_{2})\equiv(\varphi_{1}\!\vee\!\varphi)\!\vee\!\varphi_{2}
;

6.

\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\equiv\neg(\neg\varphi_{1}\vee\neg\varphi_{2})
;

7.

\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\equiv\neg(\neg\varphi_{1}\wedge\neg\varphi_{2})
;

8.

\exists^{\spcElm}\apElm\ldotp\varphi\equiv\neg(\forall^{\spcElm}\apElm\ldotp\neg\varphi)
;

9.

\forall^{\spcElm}\apElm\ldotp\varphi\equiv\neg(\exists^{\spcElm}\apElm\ldotp\neg\varphi)
.

Unlike
Q
PTL
,
GFG
-
Q
PTL
in its full generality does not enjoy the
pnf
property.
This is a consequence of the information-leak phenomenon reported in
Example
8
.
Indeed,
(\exists\apElm\ldotp\phi)\wedge\varphi\!\not\equiv\!\exists\apElm\ldotp(\phi\wedge\varphi)
and
(\forall\apElm\ldotp\phi)\vee\varphi\!\not\equiv\!\forall\apElm\ldotp(\phi\vee\varphi)
, when
\apElm\!\not\in\!{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}
.
A similar problem arises in
IF
due to
signaling
, if one let
quantifications depend on non-free variables
[
45
]
.
Fortunately, for the purposes of this work, we can focus on
pnf
formulae, since, as we shall show, behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
is powerful enough to
express all
\omega
-regular languages.

We now introduce an operator on quantifier prefixes, called
evolution
,
that, given an arbitrary hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
and one of its two
interpretations
\alpha
, computes the result
{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},\qntElm)
of the application to
{\mathfrak{X}}
of all quantifiers
{\mathtt{Q}}^{\spcElm}\apElm
occurring in a prefix
\qntElm
in that specific order.
To this aim, we need to introduce the notion of
coherence
of a quantifier
symbol
{\mathtt{Q}}\!\in\!\{\exists,\forall\}

w.r.t.
an alternation flag
\alpha\!\in\!\{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}},\allowbreak{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}\}
as follows:
{\mathtt{Q}}
is

\alpha
-coherent
if either
\alpha={\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
and
{\mathtt{Q}}=\exists
or

\alpha={\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
and
{\mathtt{Q}}=\forall
.
Essentially, the evolution operator iteratively applies the semantics of
quantifiers, as defined by Items
5a
’
and
6b
’ of Definition
7
and
Items
5b
and
6a

of Definition
3
, for all the quantifiers
{\mathtt{Q}}^{\spcElm}\apElm
in the input prefix
\qntElm
.
For a single quantifier,
{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{Q}}^{\spcElm}\apElm)
just
corresponds to the
\spcElm
-extension of
{\mathfrak{X}}
with
\apElm
, when
{\mathtt{Q}}

is
\alpha
-coherent.
On the contrary, when
{\mathtt{Q}}
is
{{\overline{\alpha}}}
-coherent, we need to dualize the

\spcElm
-extension with
\apElm
of the dual of
{\mathfrak{X}}
.

{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{Q}}^{\spcElm}\apElm)\defeq\begin{cases}{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)},&\text{ if }{\mathtt{Q}}\text{ is }\alpha\text{-coherent};\\
{{\overline{{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}}}},&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

The operator lifts naturally to an arbitrary quantification prefix
\qntElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Qn}}
as follows:

1.

{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},\epsilon)\defeq{\mathfrak{X}}
;

2.

{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{Q}}^{\spcElm}\apElm\ldotp\qntElm)\defeq\allowbreak{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{Q}}^{\spcElm}\apElm),\qntElm)
.

We also set
{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}(\qntElm)\defeq{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}(\{\{{{\varnothing}}\}\},\qntElm)
.

It is easy to show that the evolution operator is monotone
w.r.t.

\sqsubseteq
,
by simply exploiting the monotonicity of the dualization and extension operators
given in Proposition
.

Proposition 5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{HAsg}}
with
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!\sqsubseteq\!{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
and
\qntElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Qn}}
:
{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},\qntElm)\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}_{2}},\qntElm)
.

By simple structural induction on a quantifier prefix
\qntElm\in{\mathrm{Qn}}
, we
can show that a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}

\alpha
-satisfies a formula
\qntElm\phi

iff
its
\alpha
-evolution
w.r.t.

\qntElm

\alpha
-satisfies
\phi
.

Lemma 5

(Prefix Evolution)
.

Let
\qntElm\phi
be a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula with
\qntElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Qn}}
.
Then,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\qntElm\phi

iff

{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},\qntElm){{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\phi
, for all
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\phi\allowbreak\right)})
.

IV
Quantification Games

The solution of the satisfiability problem for the behavioral fragment of

GFG
-
Q
PTL
relies on the existence of a game, played by Eloise and Abelard, with
the property that Eloise wins the game
iff
the corresponding formula is indeed
satisfiable.
We provide here a general result, showing that, for any quantifier prefix

\qntElm
and Borelian property
\Psi
, there exists a game, called

quantification game
, such that Eloise wins the game
iff
the
hyperassignment obtained by evaluating the prefix, namely

{\mathsf{evl}_{\exists\forall}}(\qntElm)
, contains a set of assignments completely
included in
\Psi
.
The correctness of this result depends, in turn, on the existence of canonical
forms for the quantifier prefixes that allow one to reduce the alternations to at most one.

IV-A

Quantification Game for Sentences

To define the quantification game, we first need a few notions.
A two-player turn-based
arena

{\mathcal{A}}={{\left\langle{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{E}}}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{A}}}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathnormal{v_{I}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathit{Mv}}\right\rangle}}
is a tuple where

1.

{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{E}}}}}
and
{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{A}}}}}
are the sets of
positions
of
Eloise

and
Abelard
,
a.k.a.

Player
and
Opponent
, respectively,
with
{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{E}}}}}\cap{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{A}}}}}=\emptyset
,

2.

{\mathnormal{v_{I}}}\in{\mathrm{Ps}}\defeq{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{E}}}}}\cup{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{A}}}}}
is the
initial
position
, and

3.

{\mathit{Mv}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Ps}}\times{\mathrm{Ps}}
is the binary relation describing
all possible
moves
such that
{{\left\langle{\mathrm{Ps}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathit{Mv}}\right\rangle}}
is a sinkless
directed graph.

A
game

{\mathcal{G}}={{\left\langle{\mathcal{A}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{Ob}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{Wn}}\right\rangle}}
is a tuple where

{\mathcal{A}}
is an arena,
{\mathrm{Ob}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Ps}}
is the set of
observable
positions
, and
{\mathrm{Wn}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Ob}}^{\omega}
is the set of

observable sequences
that are
winning
for Eloise; the complement

{{\overline{{\mathrm{Wn}}}}}\defeq{\mathrm{Ob}}^{\omega}\setminus{\mathrm{Wn}}
is
winning
for
Abelard.
Eloise (
resp.
, Abelard)
wins
the game if she (
resp.
, he) has a strategy
such that, for all adversary strategies, the corresponding play induces an
observation sequence belonging (
resp.
, not belonging) to
{\mathrm{Wn}}
.
All notions necessary to formalize this intuition, as
history
,

strategy
, and
play
, are given in Appendix
-C
.​

{{\varnothing}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\bot}}}}{{\apElm[1]}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\bot}}}}{{\apElm[1]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\bot}}}}{{\apElm[2]}}
\cdots
\valElm[1]
\cdots
\vdots
{\mathtt{\bot}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\bot}}}}{{\apElm[1]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\top}}}}{{\apElm[2]}}
\cdots
\cdots
\valElm[j]
{\mathtt{\top}}
{\mathtt{\bot}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\top}}}}{{\apElm[1]}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\top}}}}{{\apElm[1]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\bot}}}}{{\apElm[2]}}
\cdots
\cdots
\vdots
{\mathtt{\bot}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\top}}}}{{\apElm[1]}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{\top}}}}{{\apElm[2]}}
\cdots
\cdots
\valElm[n]
{\mathtt{\top}}
{\mathtt{\top}}

Figure 4:
Quantification game for
\qntElm=\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm[1]\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm[2]\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm[3]\cdots
. Eloise owns the circled positions, while Abelard the squared ones.
From the total-valuation positions
\valElm[1],\ldots,\valElm[n]
, with
n=2^{{{\lvert\allowbreak{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\rvert}}}
, Abelard moves to the initial position.

Martin’s determinacy theorem
[
46
,
47
]
states that all games whose
winning condition is a Borel set in the Cantor topological space of infinite
words
[
54
]
are determined,
i.e.
, either one of the two players necessarily
wins the game.
To ensures that the quantification game we are about to define is indeed
determined, we require a form of Borelian condition that can be applied to sets
of assignments.
This determinacy requirement is crucial here, since it is tightly connected with
the fact that
GFG
-
Q
PTL
does not allow for undetermined formulae.
To this end, let
{\mathrm{Val}}\defeq{\mathrm{AP}}\pto{\mathbb{B}}
denote the set of Boolean
valuations for sets of propositions and
{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathrm{P}})\!\defeq\!{{\{\,\allowbreak\!\valElm\in{\mathrm{Val}}\!\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak\!{\mathsf{dom}\!\left(\allowbreak\valElm\allowbreak\right)}={\mathrm{P}}\!\allowbreak\,\}}}
the set of valuations for
propositions in
{\mathrm{P}}\!\subseteq\!{\mathrm{AP}}
.
Also,
\#(\valElm)\defeq{{\lvert\allowbreak{\mathsf{dom}\!\left(\allowbreak\valElm\allowbreak\right)}\allowbreak\rvert}}
.
We can now define a bijection between sets of assignments over
{\mathrm{P}}
and languages of
infinite words over the alphabet
{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathrm{P}})
.
Let
{\mathsf{wrd}}\colon{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})\to{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathrm{P}})^{\omega}
be the

word function
mapping each assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
to the
word
\wrdElm\defeq{\mathsf{wrd}}(\asgElm)\in{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathrm{P}})^{\omega}
satisfying
the equality
\asgElm(\apElm)(t)=(\wrdElm)_{t}(\apElm)
, for all
\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}}
and
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Clearly
{\mathsf{wrd}}
is a bijection.
Now, every property
\Psi\subseteq{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
,
i.e.
, every set of
assignments, uniquely induces the language of infinite words
{\mathsf{wrd}}(\Psi)\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{wrd}}(\asgElm)\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak\asgElm\in\Psi\allowbreak\,\}}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathrm{P}})^{\omega}
over the alphabet
{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathrm{P}})
.
Thus,
\Psi
is said to be
Borelian
(
resp.
, regular) if

{\mathsf{wrd}}(\Psi)
is a Borel (
resp.
, regular) set.

Given a behavioral sentence
\qntElm\psi
, let
{\mathsf{L}}(\psi)\subseteq{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)})
denote the set
of assignments satisfying the
LTL
formula
\psi
.
The quantification game
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\psi}}\defeq{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{{\mathsf{L}}(\psi)}}
is defined in Construction
1
and
exemplified in Figure
4
.

W.l.o.g.
, we assume that the prefix
\qntElm
does not contain duplicates.
The positions of the game are (partial) valuations of the propositions in

\qntElm
and each position belongs to the player corresponding to the first
quantifier in the prefix whose proposition is not defined at that position.
The initial position of the game contains the empty valuation and in the example
of Figure
4
belongs to Eloise, she being the first to play in

\qntElm
.
Obviously, the game features an infinite number of rounds.
Each round begins with the empty valuation and ends in a total valuation, after
players have chosen (jointly) a value for all the propositions.
A move in the round corresponds to a player choosing a value for the next
proposition in the prefix.
Take, for instance, position
\valElm\defeq\{\apElm[1]\mapsto{\mathtt{\bot}}\}
in the
figure, where the first proposition
\apElm[1]
has been already assigned value

{\mathtt{\bot}}
by Eloise.
From that position, Abelard first chooses a Boolean value, say
{\mathtt{\top}}
, for the
next proposition
\apElm[2]
in the prefix.
Then he moves to the position
\valElm^{\prime}\defeq\{\apElm[1]\mapsto{\mathtt{\bot}},\apElm[2]\mapsto{\mathtt{\top}}\}
, corresponding to the valuation
{\valElm}[\apElm[2]\mapsto{\mathtt{\top}}]
, obtained by extending
\valElm
with the value chosen for

\apElm[2]
.
Position
\valElm^{\prime}
belongs to Eloise, since the next quantifier
\exists\apElm[3]
in the prefix is existential.
The last positions belong to Abelard and, from there, he can only move back to
the starting position for the next turn.
By sampling any infinite sequence of rounds of the games at the positions with
total valuations, namely the observable positions, we obtain an infinite word

\wrdElm
corresponding to some assignment
\asgElm\defeq{\mathsf{wrd}^{-1}}(\wrdElm)
.
Then,
\wrdElm
is winning for Eloise
iff

\asgElm
belongs to
\Psi\!\defeq\!{\mathsf{L}}(\psi)
(
i.e.
,
\asgElm\!\models\!\psi
), while it is winning for
Abelard otherwise.
This intuition is formalized by the following construction.

Construction 1

(Quantification Game I)
.

For every quantifier prefix
\qntElm\in{\mathrm{Qn}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
and property
\Psi\subseteq{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)})
, the game
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\Psi}}\defeq\allowbreak{{\left\langle{\mathcal{A}_{\qntElm}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{Ob}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{Wn}}\right\rangle}}
with arena

{\mathcal{A}_{\qntElm}}\defeq{{\left\langle{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{E}}}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{A}}}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathnormal{v_{I}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathit{Mv}}\right\rangle}}
is
defined as prescribed in the following:

•

the set of positions
{\mathrm{Ps}}\subset{\mathrm{Val}}
contains exactly those
valuations
\valElm\in{\mathrm{Val}}
of the propositions in
{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}

that are quantified in the prefix
(\qntElm)_{<\#(\valElm)}
of
\qntElm

having length
\#(\valElm)

i.e.
,
{\mathsf{dom}\!\left(\allowbreak\valElm\allowbreak\right)}={\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak(\qntElm)_{<\#(\valElm)}\allowbreak\right)}
;

•

the set of Eloise’s positions
{\mathrm{Ps_{{\mathtt{E}}}}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Ps}}
only contains
the valuations
\valElm\in{\mathrm{Ps}}
for which the proposition quantified
in
\qntElm
at index
\#(\valElm)
is existentially quantified,
i.e.
,

(\qntElm)_{\#(\valElm)}=\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm
, for some
\apElm\in{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}
;

•

the initial position
{\mathnormal{v_{I}}}\defeq{{\varnothing}}
is just the empty
valuation;

•

the move relation
{\mathit{Mv}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Ps}}\times{\mathrm{Ps}}
contains
exactly those pairs of valuations
(\valElm[1],\valElm[2])\in{\mathrm{Ps}}\times{\mathrm{Ps}}
such that:

–

\valElm[1]\subseteq\valElm[2]
and
\#(\valElm[2])=\#(\valElm[1])+1
, or

–

\valElm[1]\in{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)})
and
\valElm[2]={{\varnothing}}
;

•

the set of observable positions
{\mathrm{Ob}}\defeq{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)})

precisely contains the valuations of all the propositions in
\qntElm
;

•

the winning condition induced by the property
\Psi
is the language of
infinite words
{\mathrm{Wn}}\defeq{\mathsf{wrd}}(\Psi)
over

{\mathrm{Val}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)})
.

The game
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\psi}}
above essentially provides a game-theoretic
version of the semantics of behavioral quantifications.
The correctness of the game is established by the following theorem.

Theorem 4

(Game-Theoretic Semantics I)
.

A behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentence
\qntElm\psi
, with
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}
,
is satisfiable (
resp.
, unsatisfiable)
iff
Eloise (
resp.
,
Abelard) wins
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\psi}}
.

The proof of this result is split into the following three steps.
First, for an arbitrary behavioral quantifier prefix
\qntElm
, we provide two
transformations,
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)
and
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)
, called

canonizations
, which allow one to reduce a behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentence

\varphi=\qntElm\psi
to the sentences
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
and

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
featuring at most one quantifier alternation.
Second, in Theorem
, we connect the winner of the game
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\psi}}
with the
satisfiability of one of the normal forms
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
and

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
, showing also that
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
implies
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
.
Finally, in Theorem
, we prove that the original sentence

\varphi
is equisatisfiable with the two normal forms.

Let us start with the definition of the two prefix canonizations based on the
following syntactic quantifier-swap operations.
Consider,
e.g.
, the formula
\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\phi
.
A naïve quantifier-swap operator would simply swap the two quantifiers that, in game-theoretic
terms, corresponds to a swap in the choices of the two players, which allows
Abelard to see Eloise’s move at the current round.
To balance this additional power, we simply restrict the universal quantifier
to be strictly behavioral, thus preventing Abelard from reading Eloise’s choice.
This leads to the formula
\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\allowbreak\forall^{\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \APSet}{\SSym: \qapElm}}\apElm\ldotp\psi
.
A symmetric swap operation would transform the formula
\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\phi
into
\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\exists^{\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \APSet}{\SSym: \apElm}}\qapElm\ldotp\phi
.
Essentially, the swap operation exchange the positions of two adjacent dual
behavioral quantifiers and restrict one of the two to be strongly behavioral

w.r.t.
the proposition of the other one.
By iteratively swapping adjacent quantifiers and adjusting the quantifier
specification accordingly, we can reduce the quantifier alternation to at most
one.

For technical convenience we use a vector notation for the quantifier prefixes:

{\mathtt{Q}}^{{{\vec{\spcElm}}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}}\ldotp\phi\defeq{\mathtt{Q}}^{({{\vec{\spcElm}}})_{0}}({{\vec{\apElm}}})_{0}\ldotp\cdots{\mathtt{Q}}^{({{\vec{\spcElm}}})_{k}}({{\vec{\apElm}}})_{k}\ldotp\phi
, where
{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\apElm}}}\allowbreak\rvert}}={{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\spcElm}}}\allowbreak\rvert}}=k+1
.
We omit the vector symbol in
{{\vec{\spcElm}}}
if this is just a sequence of

{\mathtt{B}}
or
{\mathtt{S}}
specifications and consider
{{\vec{\apElm}}}
as sets of
propositions when convenient.
We also define in a natural way the union of two quantifier specifications as
follows:

\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym 1] }{\SSym: \PSet[\SSym 1] }\cup\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym 2] }{\SSym:
\PSet[\SSym 2] }\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \PSet[\BSym 1] \cup\PSet[\BSym 2] }{\SSym:
\PSet[\SSym 1] \cup\PSet[\SSym 2] }
.

We now introduce the two syntactic transformations
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\cdot)
and

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\cdot)
that, given a behavioral quantifier prefix

\qntElm\in{\mathrm{Qn}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
, return the single-alternation ones

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)
and
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)
, by applying all the
quantifier swap operations at once.
More specifically, the function
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\cdot)
provides an

{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
-prefix
,
i.e.
, all existential quantifier precede the universal
ones, while
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\cdot)
gives us the the dual
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
-prefix
.

For the definition of
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\cdot)
, we observe that every behavioral
quantifier prefix
\qntElm
can be uniquely rewritten as
\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{0}}\ldotp(\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}\ldotp\allowbreak\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}_{k+1}}
, for some
k\!\in\!{\mathbb{N}}
and vectors
{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}
,
with
i\!\in\!{{[0\allowbreak,\allowbreak k]}}
, and
{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}
, with
i\in{{[1\allowbreak,\allowbreak k+1]}}
, where
{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}\allowbreak\rvert}},{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}\allowbreak\rvert}}\geq 1
, for all
i\in{{[1\allowbreak,\allowbreak k]}}
.
For a quantifier prefix
\qntElm
in such a form, we then define

{\mathtt{C}_{\exists\forall}}(\qntElm)\defeq(\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}})_{i=0}^{k}\ldotp(\forall^{{{\vec{\spcElm}}_{i}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}})_{i=1}^{k+1},\vskip-5.0pt

where
({{\vec{\spcElm}}_{i}})_{j}\defeq{\mathtt{B}}\cup\left<{\mathtt{S}}:{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}\cdots{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{k}}\right>
, for all
j\in{{[0\allowbreak,\allowbreak{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}\allowbreak\rvert}})}}
.

The definition of
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\cdot)
is analogous.
First, we rewrite a
prefix
\qntElm
as
\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}_{0}}\ldotp(\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{k+1}}
, for some
k\!\in\!{\mathbb{N}}
and vectors

{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}
, with
i\!\in\!{{[0\allowbreak,\allowbreak k]}}
, and
{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}
, with

i\in\allowbreak{{[1\allowbreak,\allowbreak k+1]}}
, where
{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}\allowbreak\rvert}},{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}\allowbreak\rvert}}\!\geq\!1
, for all
i\!\in\!{{[1\allowbreak,\allowbreak k]}}
.
Then, we define

{\mathtt{C}_{\forall\exists}}(\qntElm)\defeq(\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}})_{i=0}^{k}\ldotp(\exists^{{{\vec{\spcElm}}_{i}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}})_{i=1}^{k+1},

where
({{\vec{\spcElm}}_{i}})_{j}\defeq{\mathtt{B}}\cup\left<{\mathtt{S}}:{{\vec{\apElm}}_{i}}\cdots{{\vec{\apElm}}_{k}}\right>
, for all
j\in{{[0\allowbreak,\allowbreak{{\lvert\allowbreak{{\vec{\qapElm}}_{i}}\allowbreak\rvert}})}}
.

Example 10
.

Consider the behavioral quantifier prefix
\qntElm=\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\ldotp\allowbreak\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\,\rapElm\ldotp\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\sapElm\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\tapElm
.
The corresponding
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
canonical-form is
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\allowbreak\!=\!\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\qapElm\,\rapElm\,\tapElm\ldotp\!\forall^{\spcElm[][\apElm]}\!\apElm\ldotp\!\forall^{\spcElm[][\sapElm]}\!\sapElm
, where
\spcElm[][\apElm]\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \APSet}{\SSym: \qapElm\, \rapElm\,
\tapElm}
and
\spcElm[][\sapElm]\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \APSet}{\SSym: \tapElm}
.
The
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
canonical-form prefix is, instead,
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\!=\!\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}\apElm\,\sapElm\ldotp\!\exists^{\spcElm}\qapElm\,\rapElm\ldotp\!\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\tapElm
, where
\spcElm\defeq\genfrac{<}{>}{0.0pt}{}{\BSym: \APSet}{\SSym: \sapElm}
.

For the second part of the proof of Theorem
, we need to
connect the winner of
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\psi}}
with the satisfiability of (one among)

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
and
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
.
This also corresponds to showing that
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi\Rightarrow{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
.
To this end, we exploit the
\omega
-regularity of
LTL
languages, which ensures
that the game is Borelian.

Theorem 5

(Quantification Game I)
.

For each behavioral quantification prefix
\qntElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Qn}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
and
Borelian property
\Psi\!\subseteq\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)})
, the game

{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\Psi}}
​ satisfies the following two properties:

1)

if Eloise wins then
{\mathrm{E}}\subseteq\Psi
, for some
{\mathrm{E}}\in{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}({\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm))
;

2)

if Abelard wins then
{\mathrm{E}}\not\subseteq\Psi
, for all
{\mathrm{E}}\in{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}({\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm))
.

The idea of the proof is to extract, from a winning strategy of Eloise (
resp.
,
Abelard), a vector
{{\vec{{\mathsf{F}}}}}
of functors, one for each proposition
associated with that player, witnessing the existence (
resp.
, non-existence) of
a set
{\mathrm{E}}
of assignments satisfying the property
\Psi
.
More precisely, assume Eloise has a strategy
\strElm
to win the game and let

\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}}\ldotp\exists^{{{\vec{\spcElm}}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}}={\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)
be the
{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}
canonical-form of
\qntElm
.
Then, thanks to the bijection between plays
\playElm
and assignments

\asgElm
, we can operate as follows, for every round
k
and existential
proposition
\qapElm[i]
in
{{\vec{\qapElm}}}
: given Abelard’s choices up to round

k
in
\playElm
, we can extract, from Eloise’s response for
\qapElm[i]
in

\strElm
, the response to
\asgElm
at time
k
of the functor
{\mathsf{F}_{i}}
in

{{\vec{{\mathsf{F}}}}}
.
As a consequence, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({{\vec{\apElm}}})
chosen by Abelard,
Eloise’s response corresponding to the extension of
\asgElm
with
{{\vec{{\mathsf{F}}}}}

on
{{\vec{\qapElm}}}
satisfies,
i.e.
, belongs to, the property
\Psi
.
The witness
{\mathrm{E}}
is precisely the set of all those extensions.
An analogous argument applies to Abelard for the
{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}
canonical-form.
Notice that
{{\vec{{\mathsf{F}}}}}
meets the specification
{{\vec{\spcElm}}}
thanks to the
alternation of the players prescribed by
\qntElm
in each round of

{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\Psi}}
.

The final step establishes the equisatisfiability of a behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentence with its canonical forms.

Theorem 6

(Sentence Canonical Forms)
.

For every behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentence
\qntElm\psi
, with
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}
,
it holds that
\qntElm\psi
,
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
, and

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
are equisatisfiable.

Towards the proof, we can derive the chain of implications
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi\Rightarrow\qntElm\psi\Rightarrow{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
by exploiting
the following property of the evolution function.
Specifically, this asserts a total ordering
w.r.t.
the preorder
\sqsubseteq

between a behavioral quantifier prefix
\qntElm
and its two canonical forms

{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)
and
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)
that can be proved by
induction on the structure of
\qntElm
.

Proposition 6
.

\!{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{C}_{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}(\qntElm))\!\sqsubseteq\!{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},\qntElm)\!\sqsubseteq\!{\mathsf{evl}_{\alpha}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{C}_{\alpha}}(\qntElm))
, for
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
and
\qntElm\in{\mathrm{Qn}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}
, with
{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}\cap{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}=\emptyset
.

From this result, Lemma
, and Theorem
, the above
implications immediately follow.
To complete the proof, we need to show that
{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi\Rightarrow{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
holds.
Thanks to Lemma
, if
\{\!\{{{\varnothing}}\}\!\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
, then
{\mathrm{E}}\subseteq\Psi\defeq{\mathsf{L}}(\psi)
, for some
{\mathrm{E}}\in{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}({\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm))
.
Thus, by Item
of Theorem
, it follows
that Abelard loses the game
{\mathcal{G}_{\qntElm}^{\Psi}}
, which means, by determinacy,
that Eloise wins.
As a consequence of Item
of the same theorem, there
exists
{\mathrm{E}}\in{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}({\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm))
such that
{\mathrm{E}}\subseteq\Psi
.
Hence,
\{\!\{{{\varnothing}}\}\!\}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
,
again by Lemma
.

IV-B

Quantification Game for Formulae

The game defined in the previous section can easily be adapted to deal with
the satisfiability problem for behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
as shown in the
next section.
Solving the model-checking problem requires, however, a generalization of
Theorem
, connecting a suitable game with satisfiability of
arbitrary behavioral formulae
w.r.t.
a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
.
We can prove such a property under the assumption that
{\mathfrak{X}}
is

well-behaved
,
i.e.
, if

1.

{\mathfrak{X}}
is the evolution of a set of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}

w.r.t.
some
behavioral prefix
{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}
and

2.

{\mathfrak{X}}
is Borelian.

The Borelian requirement is again connected to determinacy of the underlying
game.
The behavioral requirement, instead, allows for a simple proof that leverages
the quantification game for sentences directly.
At this stage, it is not clear whether the property actually holds for arbitrary
Borelian hyperassignments.

To formalize the above assumption, we introduce the notion of
generator

for a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
as a pair
{{\left\langle{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\right\rangle}}
of

1.

a behavioral quantification prefix
{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\in{\mathrm{Qn}}
and

2.

a Borelian set of assignments
\emptyset\neq{\mathrm{X}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}\setminus{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\allowbreak\right)})

such that
{\mathfrak{X}}={\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\{{\mathrm{X}}\},{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}})
.
A hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
is
Borelian behavioral
if there
is a generator for it.
A
quantification-game schema
is a tuple
{\mathfrak{Q}}\defeq{{\left\langle{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak,\allowbreak\Psi\right\rangle}}
where

1.

{\mathfrak{X}}{\in}{\mathrm{HAsg}}
is Borelian behavioral,

2.

\qntElm{\in}{\mathrm{Qn}}
is behavioral,

3.

\Psi\!\subseteq\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}\cup{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)})
is Borelian,
and

4.

{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}\cap{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}=\emptyset
.

The idea behind the game-theoretic construction reported below is quite simple.
Given a generator
{{\left\langle{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\right\rangle}}
for a behavioral
hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
, we force the two players to simulate the given

{\mathfrak{X}}
by playing according to the prefix
{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}
, once Abelard has
arbitrarily chosen the values of the atomic propositions
{{\vec{\apElm}}}
over
which the set of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}
is defined.
Since
{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\forall{{\vec{\apElm}}}\,)=\{{\mathrm{Asg}}({{\vec{\apElm}}})\}
and

{\mathrm{X}}\subseteq{\mathrm{Asg}}({{\vec{\apElm}}}\,)
, it is clear that
{\mathfrak{X}}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\forall{{\vec{\apElm}}}\ldotp{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}})
.
Thus, if Eloise wins the game, she can ensure a given temporal property.
Notice, however, that we gave Abelard the freedom to cheat and choose arbitrary
values for
{{\vec{\apElm}}}
.
Thus, in principle, Eloise could be able to satisfy the property while loosing
the game, since Abelard can choose assignments over
{{\vec{\apElm}}}
that do not
belong to
{\mathrm{X}}
.
To remedy this, we add all those assignments to Eloise’s winning set, thus
deterring Abelard from cheating.

Construction 2

(Quantification Game II)
.

For a quantification-game schema
{\mathfrak{Q}}\defeq{{\left\langle{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak,\allowbreak\Psi\right\rangle}}
, we say that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a
{\mathfrak{Q}}
-game if there is a generator
{{\left\langle{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\right\rangle}}
for
{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
{\mathcal{G}}\defeq{\mathcal{G}_{{{\widehat{\qntElm}}}}^{{{\widehat{\Psi}}}}}
, where

•

{{\widehat{\qntElm}}}\defeq\forall{{\vec{\apElm}}}\ldotp{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\ldotp\qntElm
and

•

{{\widehat{\Psi}}}\defeq\Psi\cup{{\{\,\allowbreak\asgElm\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak\asgElm\rst{{{\vec{\apElm}}}}\,\not\in{\mathrm{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}
,

with
{{\vec{\apElm}}}\defeq{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}\setminus{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\widetilde{\qntElm}}}\allowbreak\right)}
and

{\mathrm{P}}\defeq{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak\right)}\cup{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
.

The quantification-game schema for

\qntElm\psi
, with
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}
, and a hyperassignment
{\mathfrak{X}}
is the
tuple
{\mathfrak{Q}_{\qntElm\psi}^{{\mathfrak{X}}}}\defeq{{\left\langle{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak\qntElm\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathsf{L}}(\psi)\right\rangle}}
.

Theorem 7

(Game-Theoretic Semantics II)
.

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\qntElm\psi

iff
Eloise wins every

{\mathfrak{Q}_{\qntElm\psi}^{{\mathfrak{X}}}}
-game, for all behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formulae

\qntElm\psi
, with
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}
, and Borelian behavioral hyperassignments
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\psi\allowbreak\right)})
.

The proof is similar to the one of
Theorem
and
uses the following result, which generalizes
Theorem
.

Theorem 8

(Quantification Game II)
.

Every
{\mathfrak{Q}}
-game
{\mathcal{G}}
, for some quantification-game
schema
{\mathfrak{Q}}\defeq{{\left\langle\qntElm\allowbreak,\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak,\allowbreak\Psi\right\rangle}}
, satisfies the
following two properties:

1)

if Eloise wins then
{\mathrm{E}}\subseteq\Psi
, for some
{\mathrm{E}}\in{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm))
;

2)

if Abelard wins then
{\mathrm{E}}\not\subseteq\Psi
, for all
{\mathrm{E}}\in{\mathsf{evl}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}({\mathfrak{X}},{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm))
.

Theorem
, together with Proposition
,
lifts Theorem
to formulae, and allows us to obtain
Theorem
.

Theorem 9

(Formula Canonical Forms)
.

For every behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula
\qntElm\psi
, with
\psi\in\text{LTL$$}
,
it holds that
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\qntElm\psi

iff

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}(\qntElm)\psi

iff

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}{\mathtt{C}_{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}(\qntElm)\psi
, for all Borelian behavioral hyperassignments

{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\qntElm\psi\allowbreak\right)})
.

V
Decision Problems & Expressiveness

The results of the previous section can be exploited to solve optimally the
decision problems for behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
.
More specifically, we can use the game of Constructions
1
for
the satisfiability problem, and the game of Constructions
2
for
the model-checking one.
We also discuss the expressiveness relationship between
Q
PTL
and behavioral

GFG
-
Q
PTL
, showing, by means of a classic encoding of automata into logic, that
they have the same expressive power, though
Q
PTL
is non-elementary more
succinct then
GFG
-
Q
PTL
.

V-A

Decision Procedures

The first step in deciding the satisfiability problem is to derive from a
behavioral sentence
\varphi=\qntElm\psi
a
parity
game

[
51
,
9
]
that is won by Eloise
iff

\varphi
is satisfiable.
To do that, we first construct a deterministic parity automaton
{\mathcal{D}_{\psi}}

for the
LTL
formula
\psi
, by combining the Vardi-Wolper
construction
[
66
]
with the Safra-like translation from Büchi to parity
acceptance condition
[
55
]
.
We then compute the synchronous product of the arena
{\mathcal{A}_{\qntElm}}
of
Construction
1
with
{\mathcal{D}_{\psi}}
, where the automaton
component changes its state only when Abelard moves from observable positions
containing a full valuation of the propositions.
This valuation is, then, read by the transition function of
{\mathcal{D}_{\psi}}
to
determine its successor state.
The resulting game simulates both the quantification game and the automaton, so
that Eloise wins
iff
the play satisfies
\psi
.

Theorem 10

(Satisfiability Game)
.

For every behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentence
\varphi
there is a parity game,
with
{\mathrm{O}\!\left(\allowbreak 2^{2^{{{\lvert\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\rvert}}}}\allowbreak\right)}
positions and

{\mathrm{O}\!\left(\allowbreak 2^{{{\lvert\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\rvert}}}\allowbreak\right)}
priorities, won by Eloise
iff

\varphi
is
satisfiable.

We can then obtain an upper bound on the complexity of the problem from the fact
that parity games can be solved in time polynomial in the number of positions
and exponential in that of the priorities
[
8
,
10
,
70
]
.
For the lower bound, instead, we observe that the
reactive synthesis
problem

[
58
]
of an
LTL
formula
\psi
can be reduced to the
satisfiability of a sentence of the form
\forall^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\apElm}}}\ldotp\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}{{\vec{\qapElm}}}\ldotp\psi
, where
{{\vec{\apElm}}}
and

{{\vec{\qapElm}}}
denote, respectively, the input and output signals of the
desired system.

Theorem 11

(Satisfiability)
.

The satisfiability problem for behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
sentences is 2
ExpTime-complete
.

For the universal (
resp.
, existential) model-checking problem, given a Kripke
structure
{\mathcal{K}}
, we ask whether
{\mathcal{K}}\models\varphi
, in the sense that

{\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathcal{K}}}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\varphi
(
resp.
,
{\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathcal{K}}}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\varphi
) holds, where
{\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathcal{K}}}}\defeq\allowbreak\{{\mathsf{wrd}^{-1}}({\mathsf{L}}({\mathcal{K}}))\}
is the hyperassignment obtained by collecting all
those assignments
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\right)})
over the propositions
of
{\mathcal{K}}
for which the infinite word
{\mathsf{wrd}}(\asgElm)
belongs to the

\omega
-regular language
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
generated by
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Obviously,
{\mathfrak{X}_{{\mathcal{K}}}}
is a Borelian behavioral hyperassignment.
As a consequence, Construction
2
applies.
Thus, we can adopt the same synchronous product described above between the
arena of the game and the union of the two automata
{\mathcal{D}_{\psi}}
and

{\mathcal{N}_{{{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}}}}}
, where
{\mathcal{D}_{\psi}}
is obtained from the formula

\psi
, while
{\mathcal{N}_{{{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}}}}}
is a co-safety automaton of size linear in

{{\lvert\allowbreak{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\rvert}}
, recognizing the complement of
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Theorem 12

(Model-Checking Game)
.

For every Kripke structure
{\mathcal{K}}
and behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula

\varphi
, with
{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}\!\subseteq\!{\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\right)}
, there is a parity
game, with
{\mathrm{O}\!\left(\allowbreak 2^{2^{{{\lvert\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\rvert}}}}{{\lvert\allowbreak{\mathcal{K}}\allowbreak\rvert}}\allowbreak\right)}
positions and

{\mathrm{O}\!\left(\allowbreak 2^{{{\lvert\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\rvert}}}\allowbreak\right)}
priorities, won by Eloise
iff

{\mathcal{K}}\!\models\!\varphi
.

Upper bounds
w.r.t.
both formula and model complexity, and the lower bound
w.r.t.
formula complexity, are proved as in the case of the satisfiability problem.
The lower bound
w.r.t.
model complexity is proved by reducing from reachability
games
[
36
]
.

Theorem 13

(Model-Checking)
.

The model-checking problem for behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
has 2
ExpTime-complete
formula
complexity and
PTime-complete
model complexity.

V-B

Expressive Power

We conclude the work by discussing the expressive power of the behavioral
fragment of
GFG
-
Q
PTL
, showing that it precisely corresponds to the

\omega
-regular languages.
Similarly to Example
9
, consider an arbitrary deterministic
parity automaton
{\mathcal{D}}
with
k
states over an alphabet
2^{{\mathrm{P}}}
, with

{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
.
Via the standard technique of encoding the existence of an accepting run, we
can construct an
LTL
formula
\psi
, over the set of propositions
{\mathrm{P}}\cup\{\sapElm[1],\ldots,\sapElm[k]\}
, such that the existential projection on

{\mathrm{P}}
of the language
{\mathsf{L}}(\psi)
coincides with the language

{\mathsf{L}}({\mathcal{D}})
recognized by
{\mathcal{D}}
.
Since
{\mathcal{D}}
is deterministic, this projection is clearly behavioral.
Hence, the behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula
\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\sapElm[1],\ldots,\exists^{{\mathtt{B}}}\sapElm[k]\ldotp\psi
is satisfied by an hyperassignment
\{\{\asgElm\}\}

iff

{\mathsf{wrd}}(\asgElm)\!\in\!{\mathsf{L}}({\mathcal{D}})
.
Since every
Q
PTL
formula can be translated into an equivalent nondeterministic
Büchi automaton
[
64
]
, which in turn can be determinized into a parity
one
[
55
]
, we obtain that for every
Q
PTL
formula, there is an
equivalent behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
one.
The converse holds as well.
Indeed, the satisfiability game
{\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}}
can be transformed into a
isomorphic alternating parity word automaton
{\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}}
, in the usual
way, which can then be reduced to a nondeterministic parity automaton

{\mathcal{N}_{\varphi}}

[
52
]
.
The emptiness of
{\mathcal{N}_{\varphi}}
can then be encoded into a
Q
PTL
sentence.
A similar reasoning applies also to formulae.

Theorem 14

(Expressiveness)
.

Q
PTL
and behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
are equi-expressive.

Clearly,
Q
PTL
is also non-elementary more succinct than behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
.
Indeed, the satisfiability problem for
Q
PTL
sentences with alternation of
quantifiers
k
is
(k-1)
-
ExpSpace-complete

[
64
]
, while behavioral

GFG
-
Q
PTL
is decidable in 2
ExpTime
, so no elementary reduction exists.

Theorem 15

(Succinctness)
.

Q
PTL
is non-elementary more succinct than behavioral
GFG
-
Q
PTL
.

VI
Discussion

We have introduced a novel semantics for
Q
PTL
extending Hodges’ team
semantics for Hintikka and Sandu’s logic of imperfect information
IF
in a
non-trivial way.
On the one hand, the new semantic setting can express games with both
symmetric and asymmetric restrictions on the players.
On the other hand, it allows for encoding behavioral constraints on the
quantified propositions, connecting the underlying logic with the
game-theoretic notion of behavioral strategies.
Based on this semantics, the extension of
Q
PTL
with constraints on the
functional dependencies among propositions, called
GFG
-
Q
PTL
, has
surprisingly interesting properties.
For one, its behavioral fragment enables reducing the solution of two-player
zero-sum games to the decision problems for the logic.
Indeed, the deep connection with behavioral strategies ensures that
satisfiable formulae of the logic express linear time properties that can
always be realized by means of actual strategies.
This fragment also enjoys good computational properties, being 2
ExpTime-complete
for
both satisfiability and model-checking.
It is also very expressive, being equivalent to, though less succinct than,

Q
PTL
, hence able to describe all
\omega
-regular properties.
Second, the behavioral semantics also bears a connection to good-for-game
automata, allowing to naturally express the property of being a GFG automata,
the significance of which is probably worth investigating further.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first attempt to provide a
compositional account of behavioral constraints.
We believe the generality and flexibility of the semantic settings opens up
the possibility of a systematic investigation of the impact of this type of
constraints in quantified temporal logics, such as
Q
CTL

[
15
,
42
]
,
Hyper
LTL
/
CTL*

[
6
,
12
,
11
,
14
,
7
]
, Coordination
Logic
[
13
]
, and Strategy Logic
[
5
,
48
]
.

-A

Proofs for Section
II

Proposition 0
.

{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
and
{\mathfrak{X}}\equiv{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
, for all
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
.

Proof.

To begin with, we show that
{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
.
By definition of
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, for every
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
there is a function
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such that
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}={{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}
.
Now, consider an arbitrary
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
and define
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
as:

{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})={\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}})
for every

{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
.
Notice that
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
, for every

{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, and thus
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\right)}
.
Therefore, we have that
{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}\in{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
.
To conclude the proof, we are left to show that
{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}={\mathrm{X}}
holds as well.
First, observe that
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})={\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}})\in{\mathrm{X}}
holds for every
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, implying
{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}\subseteq{\mathrm{X}}
.
In order to show the converse inclusion (
{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}\supseteq{\mathrm{X}}
), consider an
arbitrary

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
and a function
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm}}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such that
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm}}}({\mathrm{X}})=\asgElm
.
Let
{\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm}}}({\mathrm{X}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}
.
It holds
{\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
.
Since
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm})={\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathrm{X}}_{\asgElm}}}({\mathrm{X}})=\asgElm
, we have that
\asgElm\in{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}
and, since
\asgElm
was chosen arbitrarily, we
conclude
{{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}\supseteq{\mathrm{X}}
.

Observe that, straightforwardly,
{\mathfrak{X}}\subseteq{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
implies
{\mathfrak{X}}\sqsubseteq{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
.

Let us turn now to proving
{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Let
{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}\in{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
.
By definition of
{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
, there is a function

{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\right)}
such
that

{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}={{\{\,\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\,\}}}
.
Towards a contradiction, assume that for every
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
there
is
\asgElm_{{\mathrm{X}}}\in{\mathrm{X}}\setminus{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}
.
Let us define
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
as:
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({\mathrm{X}})=\asgElm_{{\mathrm{X}}}
for every

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
.
Notice that
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}

Thus,
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\defeq{{\{\,\allowbreak\asgElm_{{\mathrm{X}}}\allowbreak\allowbreak\mid\allowbreak\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\,\}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
and
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\cap{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}=\emptyset
.
However,
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\in{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}}\cap{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
, thus rising a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 0

(Dualization)
.

The following hold true, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
and hyperassignments

{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
.

1.

Statements
and
are equivalent:

(a)

there exists
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
,
for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
,
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for
some
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

2.

Statements
and
are equivalent:

(a)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for some

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

there exists
{\mathrm{X}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

(
\ref{lmm:flp(ea:org)}\Rightarrow\ref{lmm:flp(ea:dlt)}
)

By
, there is
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that

\asgElm\models\varphi
holds for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.
By definition of
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, for every
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
there is
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}
such that

{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}})\in{\mathrm{X}}
and
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}=\{{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}}):{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}\}
;
by
,
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}})\models\varphi
;
since, in addition,
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}}({\mathrm{X}})\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
, the
thesis
holds.

(
\ref{lmm:flp(ea:dlt)}\Rightarrow\ref{lmm:flp(ea:org)}
)

By
, for every
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}

there is

\asgElm_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
such that

\asgElm_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}\models\varphi
.
Consider function
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\allowbreak\right)}
defined
as:
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})=\asgElm_{{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}}
, for every

{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
.
By definition of
{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
, we have
that
\{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}):{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\}\in{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}
.
By Proposition
, it holds
{{\overline{{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}}}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}}
, which means that there is
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
with
{\mathrm{X}}\subseteq\{{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}):{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}\}
.
Since, by construction,
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}})\models\varphi
for every

{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
, the thesis holds.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ae:org)}\Leftrightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ae:par)}
)

By
statement
of this lemma, we have that

is false if and only if

is false
(
\mathit{not}\ \ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}\Leftrightarrow\mathit{not}\ \ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}
, for short).
By instantiating, in this last equivalence,
\varphi
with

\neg\varphi
, we have

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
, where

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}
and
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
are abbreviations
for,
respectively:

–

for all sets of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, there exists an assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that

\asgElm\not\models\neg\varphi
;

–

there exists a set of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
such
that, for all assignments
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
, it holds that
\asgElm\not\models\neg\varphi
.

By applying semantics of negation, it is straightforward to see that

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}
and
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
correspond to

and
, respectively, hence
the
thesis.
∎

Lemma 0

(Boolean Connectives)
.

The following hold true, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi_{1}
and

\varphi_{2}
and hyperassignments
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathrm{P}})
,
with
{\mathrm{P}}\defeq\allowbreak{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi_{1}\allowbreak\right)}\cup{\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi_{2}\allowbreak\right)}
.

1.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

there is
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\!\models\!\varphi_{1}\!\wedge\!\varphi_{2}
, for all
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

for each
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
, there are

i\in\{1,2\}
and
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi_{i}
, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

2.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\asgElm\!\models\!\varphi_{1}\!\vee\!\varphi_{2}
, for some
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
;​

(b)

there is
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\!\in\!{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such
that, for all
i\in\{1,2\}
and
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
,

\asgElm\!\models\!\varphi_{i}
, for some
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}\Rightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}
)

Let
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
be such that
\asgElm\models\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
holds for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
and consider an
arbitrary pair
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
.
Since
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})
is a partition of
{\mathfrak{X}}
, either

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
or
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
: in the former case,
let
i=1
;
in the latter, let
i=2
.
Since
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
and
\asgElm\models\varphi_{i}
holds
for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
, the thesis holds.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}\Rightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}
)

Consider the hyperassignment
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}}=\{{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}:\forall\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}\ .\ \asgElm\models\varphi_{1}\}

and the
pair
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\defeq{\mathfrak{X}}\setminus{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\defeq{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
.
Observe that, by definition of
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
, there is no
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi_{1}
holds for every

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.
Thus, by
, there must exist
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi_{2}
holds for every

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.
By definition of
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
, it also holds that
\asgElm\models\varphi_{1}
for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
, hence the thesis.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ae:org)}\Leftrightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ae:par)}
)

By
statement
of this lemma, we have that

is false if and only if

is false
(
\mathit{not}\ \ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}\Leftrightarrow\mathit{not}\ \ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}
, for short).
By instantiating, in this last equivalence,
\varphi_{1}
with

\neg\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
with
\neg\varphi_{2}
, we have

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
, where

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}
and
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
are abbreviations
for,
respectively:

–

for all sets of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, there exists an assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that

\asgElm\not\models\neg\varphi_{1}\wedge\neg\varphi_{2}
;

–

there exists a pair of hyperassignments
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such that, for all indexes
i\in\{1,2\}
and sets of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
, there exists an
assignment

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
for which it holds that
\asgElm\not\models\neg\varphi_{i}
.

By applying semantics of negation and De Morgan’s laws, it is
straightforward to see that
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}
and

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
correspond to
and

, respectively, hence the thesis. ∎

Lemma 0

(Hyperassignment Extensions)
.

The following hold true, for all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
, atomic propositions

\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
, and hyperassignments
{\mathfrak{X}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)}\setminus\{\apElm\})
.

1.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

there is
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\models\exists\apElm\ldotp\varphi
, for all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

there is
{\mathrm{X}}\!\in\!{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
such that
\asgElm\!\!\models\!\!\varphi
, for all
\asgElm\!\in\!{\mathrm{X}}
.

2.

Statements
and
are
equivalent:

(a)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\asgElm\models\forall\apElm\ldotp\varphi
, for some
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

(b)

for all
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
,
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for some
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}\Rightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}
)

Let

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
be such that
\asgElm\models\exists\apElm.\varphi

holds for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
. By semantics
(Def.
2
, item

3a
), for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
, there is a
temporal
function
{\mathsf{f}}_{\asgElm}\in{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{B}}
such that

{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{f}}_{\asgElm}]\models\varphi
.
Let
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)})
be such that

{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm)={\mathsf{f}}_{\asgElm}
for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
and let

{\mathrm{X}}_{{\mathsf{F}}}=\{{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm)]:\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}\}
.
Since
{\mathrm{X}}_{{\mathsf{F}}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
and
\asgElm\models\varphi
holds for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}_{{\mathsf{F}}}
, the thesis
holds.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}\Rightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}
)

Let

{\mathrm{X}}_{{\mathsf{F}}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
be such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
holds for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}_{{\mathsf{F}}}
.
By definition of
{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
, there are
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
and
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)})
such that
{\mathrm{X}}_{{\mathsf{F}}}=\{{\asgElm}[\apElm\mapsto{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm)]:\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}\}
.
Clearly, by semantics (Def.
2
, item

3a
),
\asgElm\models\exists\apElm.\varphi

holds for
every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
, hence the thesis.

(
\ref{lmm:blncon(ae:org)}\Leftrightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ae:par)}
)

By
statement
of this lemma, we have that

is false if and only if

is false
(
\mathit{not}\ \ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}\Leftrightarrow\mathit{not}\ \ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}
, for short).
By instantiating, in this last equivalence,
\varphi
with

\neg\varphi
, we have
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
, where
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}
and

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
are abbreviations for, respectively:

–

for all sets of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, there exists an
assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that
\asgElm\not\models\exists\apElm.\neg\varphi
;

–

for all sets of assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
, there exists an assignment
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that

\asgElm\not\models\neg\varphi
.

By applying semantics of negation and duality of
\exists
and

\forall
, it is straightforward to see that
\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:org)}^{\prime}

and

\ref{lmm:blncon(ea:par)}^{\prime}
correspond to
and

, respectively, hence the thesis. ∎

Theorem 0

(Semantics Adequacy I)
.

For all
Q
PTL
formulae
\varphi
and hyperassignments
\!{\mathfrak{X}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
:

1.

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\>\varphi

iff
there exists a set of
assignments
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for
all
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
;

2.

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\>\varphi

iff
, for all sets of assignments

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, it holds that
\asgElm\models\varphi
, for some

\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
.

Proof.

Both claims 1 and 2 are proved together, by induction on the structure of
the formula.

(base case)

If
\varphi\in\text{LTL$$}
, then the
claims immediately follows from the semantics
(Definition
3
,
item
1
).

(inductive step)

If
\varphi=\neg\psi
, then we have, by
semantics,

{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if and only if
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\not\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\psi
.
If
\alpha=\exists\forall
, then, by inductive hypothesis, it is not
the case that for every
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
there is
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}

such
that
\asgElm{{\,\models}}\psi
, which amounts to say that there is

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
it holds

\asgElm{{\,\not\models}}\psi
, from which the thesis follows.
If, instead,
\alpha=\forall\exists
, then, by inductive hypothesis,
there is no
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
such that for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}

it holds

\asgElm{{\,\models}}\psi
, which amounts to say that for every

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
there is
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that
\asgElm{{\,\not\models}}\psi
, from which the thesis follows.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
and
\alpha=\exists\forall
,
then we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if
and only
if for every
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}

it holds true that
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi_{1}
or it holds true that
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi_{2}
.
By inductive hypothesis, this amounts to say that for every

({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
there is
i\in\{1,2\}
and

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
such that for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
it holds

\asgElm{{\,\models}}\varphi_{i}
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
and
\alpha=\forall\exists
,
then we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if
and only
if
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\varphi
.
By proceeding as before, i.e., by applying semantics, inductive
hypothesis, and Lemma
, item
, we
have that
there is
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
such that for every

{{\overline{\asgElm}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
it holds
{{\overline{\asgElm}}}{{\,\models}}\varphi
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
and
\alpha=\forall\exists
,
then we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if
and only
if there is
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such that

{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\neq\emptyset
implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi_{1}

and
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\neq\emptyset
implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\varphi_{2}
.
By inductive hypothesis, this amounts to say that there is
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}}\allowbreak\right)}
such that for every
i\in\{1,2\}
and

{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
there is
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
for which it holds

\asgElm{{\,\models}}\varphi_{i}
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
and
\alpha=\exists\forall
,
then we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if
and only
if
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\varphi
.
By proceeding as before, i.e., by applying semantics, inductive
hypothesis, and Lemma
, item
, we
have that
for every
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
there is
{{\overline{\asgElm}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
such that
{{\overline{\asgElm}}}{{\,\models}}\varphi
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\exists\apElm.\psi
and
\alpha=\exists\forall
, then
we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if and
only if

{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\psi
.
By inductive hypothesis, this amounts to say that there is
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
such that for every
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
it
holds

\asgElm{{\,\models}}\psi
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\exists\apElm.\psi
and
\alpha=\forall\exists
,
then we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if
and only
if
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\varphi
.
By proceeding as before, i.e., by applying semantics, inductive
hypothesis, and Lemma
, item
, we
have that
there is
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
such that for every

{{\overline{\asgElm}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
it holds
{{\overline{\asgElm}}}{{\,\models}}\varphi
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\forall\apElm.\psi
and
\alpha=\forall\exists
, then
we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if and
only if

{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\psi
.
By inductive hypothesis, this amounts to say that for every
{\mathrm{X}}\in{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
there is
\asgElm\in{\mathrm{X}}
such that

\asgElm{{\,\models}}\psi
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
.

If
\varphi=\forall\apElm.\psi
and
\alpha=\exists\forall
,
then we have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}}{{\,\models}^{\alpha}}\>\varphi
if
and only
if
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}{{\,\models}^{{{\overline{\alpha}}}}}\>\varphi
.
By proceeding as before, i.e., by applying semantics, inductive
hypothesis, and Lemma
, item
, we
have that for every
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}}}}}
there is

{{\overline{\asgElm}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}}}}}
such that
{{\overline{\asgElm}}}{{\,\models}}\varphi
.
The thesis follows from Lemma
,
item
. ∎

-B

Proofs for Section
III

Proposition 0
.

Let
{\mathrm{P}}\subseteq{\mathrm{AP}}
,
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\in{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
,

\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
\asgElm[1]\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[2]

iff
the following hold
true:

1.

\asgElm[1](\qapElm)=\asgElm[2](\qapElm)
, for all
\qapElm\in{\mathrm{P}}\setminus({\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\cup{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}})
;

2.

\asgElm[1](\apElm)(t)=\asgElm[2](\apElm)(t)
, for all
t\leq k
and

\apElm\in({\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\cap{\mathrm{P}})\setminus{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
;

3.

\asgElm[1](\apElm)(t)=\asgElm[2](\apElm)(t)
, for all
t<k
and

\apElm\in{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}\cap{\mathrm{P}}
.

Proof.

Assume
\asgElm[1]\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[2]
, i.e.,
\asgElm[1]=\asgElm[][(1)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(2)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\ldots\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(r)]=\asgElm[2]
, for some
\asgElm[][(1)],\ldots,\asgElm[][(r)]
, with
r\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
(observe that

\asgElm[1]=\asgElm[2]
if
r=1
).

We prove, by induction on
r
, that
items
–
hold.
If
r=1
, then the claim follows trivially.
Let
r>1
.
Since
\asgElm[][(1)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(2)]
, we have that

–
hold when instantiated with

\asgElm[][(1)]
and
\asgElm[][(2)]
, by Definition
4
.
Moreover, by inductive hypothesis,

–
hold when instantiated with

\asgElm[][(2)]
and
\asgElm[][(r)]
.
The claim follows by transitivity of

–
.

Now, in order to prove the converse direction, assume that
items
–
hold.
Let
{{\{\,\allowbreak\apElm[1],\ldots,\apElm[r]\allowbreak\,\}}}
be an enumeration of
{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{B}}}}\cup{\mathrm{P}_{{\mathtt{S}}}}
and define
\asgElm[][(1)]\defeq\asgElm[1]
and

\asgElm[][(i+1)]\defeq\asgElm[][(i)][][\apElm[i]\mapsto\asgElm[2](\apElm[i])]
for
i\in[1,\ldots,r]
.
It is not difficult to convince oneself that
\asgElm[1]=\asgElm[][(1)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(2)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\ldots\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(r+1)]=\asgElm[2]
holds, hence
\asgElm[1]\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[2]
.
∎

Proposition 0
.

If
\asgElm[1]\!\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\!\asgElm[2]
then

{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(k)\!=\!{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(k)
, for all
\asgElm[1],\asgElm[2]\!\in\!{\mathrm{Asg}}({\mathrm{P}})
,
\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
,
k\!\in\!{\mathbb{N}}
,
and
{\mathsf{F}}\!\in\!{\mathrm{Fnc}_{\spcElm}}({\mathrm{P}})
.

Proof.

Assume
\asgElm[1]\approx_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[2]
, i.e.,
\asgElm[1]=\asgElm[][(1)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(2)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\ldots\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(r)]=\asgElm[2]
, for some
\asgElm[][(1)],\ldots,\asgElm[][(r)]
, with
r\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
(observe that

\asgElm[1]=\asgElm[2]
if
r=1
).

We prove, by induction on
r
, that
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[1])(k)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[2])(k)
.
If
r=1
, then the claim follows trivially.
Let
r>1
.
Since
\asgElm[][(1)]\sim_{\spcElm}^{k}\asgElm[][(2)]
and
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}_{\spcElm}}({\mathrm{P}})
, we have that
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[][(1)])(k)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[][(2)])(k)
.
Moreover, by inductive hypothesis,
{\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[][(2)])(k)={\mathsf{F}}(\asgElm[][(r)])(k)
.
The claim follows by transitivity.
∎

Proposition 0
.

Let
\apElm\in{\mathrm{AP}}
,
\spcElm\in{\mathrm{\mathbbo{\Theta}}}
, and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}}
with
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
:

1.

{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}}}}\sqsubseteq{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}}
;

2.

{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\sqsubseteq{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
.

Proof.

Proof of point
1)
. Assume
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!\sqsubseteq\!{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!
and let
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}}}}
.
We have to show that there exists
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}_{1}}}}}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}}
such
that
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}_{1}}}}}\subseteq{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}}}
.
By
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
, there is a function
f:{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
, such that
f({\mathrm{X}_{1}})\subseteq{\mathrm{X}_{1}}
.
By definition of
{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}}}}
, we have that
{{\overline{{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}}}={\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}}\allowbreak\right)}
for some
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\allowbreak\right)}
.

Now, define
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}
as
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}({\mathrm{X}_{1}})\defeq{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}}(f({\mathrm{X}_{1}}))
for every
{\mathrm{X}_{1}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
.
Clearly,
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}\in{\mathrm{Chc}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}
, as
{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}({\mathrm{X}_{1}})={\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}}(f({\mathrm{X}_{1}}))\in f({\mathrm{X}_{1}})\subseteq{\mathrm{X}_{1}}
, and thus

{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}\in{{\overline{{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}}}}
.
The thesis follows from the fact that
{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}\subseteq{\mathsf{img}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{2}}\allowbreak\right)}={{\overline{{\mathrm{X}_{2}}}}}
.

Proof of point
2)
. Assume
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!\sqsubseteq\!{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!
and let
{\mathrm{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}\in{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
.
We have to show that there exists
{\mathrm{X}_{2}^{{}^{\prime}}}\in{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
such that
{\mathrm{X}_{2}^{{}^{\prime}}}\subseteq{\mathrm{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}
.
By definition of
{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
, we have that

{\mathrm{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}={\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}_{1}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
for some
{\mathrm{X}_{1}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
and
{\mathsf{F}}\in{\mathrm{Fnc}_{\spcElm}}({\mathsf{ap}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)})
.
By
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
and
{\mathrm{X}_{1}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
, we have
that there is
{\mathrm{X}_{2}}\in{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
such that
{\mathrm{X}_{2}}\subseteq{\mathrm{X}_{1}}
.

It clearly holds that
{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}_{2}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\subseteq{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}_{1}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
.
The thesis follows, since
{\mathsf{ext}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathrm{X}_{2}},{\mathsf{F}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}\in{\mathsf{ext}_{\spcElm}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}},\apElm\allowbreak\right)}
.
∎

Theorem 0

(Hyperassignment Refinement)
.

Let
\varphi
be a
GFG
-
Q
PTL
formula and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\in{\mathrm{HAsg}_{\subseteq}}({\mathsf{free}\!\left(\allowbreak\varphi\allowbreak\right)})
with
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
.
Then,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\!\varphi
implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\exists\forall}}}}\!\varphi
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\!\varphi

implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!{{\,\models}^{{\mathtt{\forall\exists}}}}\!\varphi
.

Proof.

Assume
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\!\sqsubseteq\!{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
.
Thus, there is a function
f:{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}
, such that

f({\mathrm{X}_{1}})\subseteq{\mathrm{X}_{1}}
.
The claim is proved by induction on the structure of the formula and the
alternation flags.

(base case)

If
\varphi\in\text{LTL$$}
, then the
claims immediately follows from the semantics
(Definition
3
,
item
1
).

(inductive step)

–

(
\varphi=\neg\psi
) We have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{\exists\forall}}\>\varphi
if and only if
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\not\models}^{\forall\exists}}\>\psi
.
By inductive hypothesis, this implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\not\models}^{\forall\exists}}\>\psi
, which amounts to
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{\exists\forall}}\>\varphi
.

On the other hand, we also have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\models}^{\forall\exists}}\>\varphi
if and only if
{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}{{\,\not\models}^{\exists\forall}}\>\psi
.
By inductive hypothesis, this implies
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\not\models}^{\exists\forall}}\>\psi
, which amounts to
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{\forall\exists}}\>\varphi
.

–

(
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
)
We have, by semantics,
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}{{\,\models}^{\exists\forall}}\>\varphi
if and only if for every
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}},{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}

it holds true that
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}}{{\,\models}^{\exists\forall}}\varphi_{1}
or it holds true that
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}{{\,\models}^{\exists\forall}}\varphi_{2}
.
Now, consider
({\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{{}^{\prime}}},{\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}\allowbreak\right)}

and
({\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}},{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}})\in{\mathsf{par}\!\left(\allowbreak{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\allowbreak\right)}
, where
